<!-- head-1: body_md -->
For an integer $N\geq1$, let $[N]:=\{1,\ldots,N\}$, and let
$[a]_+:=\max\{a,0\}$ denote the positive part of $a\in\mathbb R$.
For a nonempty closed convex set $\mathcal C\subseteq\mathbb R^d$,
$\Pi_{\mathcal C}$ denotes the Euclidean projection onto $\mathcal C$ and
$\operatorname{cl}(\mathcal C)$ its closure. 
The tangent and normal cones to the set $\mathcal C$ at a point $x \in\mathcal C$ are
$T_{\mathcal C}(x) :=
\operatorname{cl}
\left\{
\lambda(y-x):
y\in\mathcal C,\ \lambda\geq0
\right\}$ and 
$N_{\mathcal C}(x)
:=\left\{v:\langle v,y-x\rangle\leq0,\ \forall y\in\mathcal C\right\}.$
We write
$\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal C):=
\sup_{x,y\in\mathcal C}\|x-y\|_2\,,
\operatorname{dist}(x,\mathcal C)
:=\inf_{y\in\mathcal C}\|x-y\|_2$ 
where $\|\cdot\|_2$ is the Euclidean 2-norm. 
For a block vector
$v=(v_1,\ldots,v_N)$, with
$v_i\in\mathbb R^{d_i}$, we use
$\|v\|_{2,1}:=\sum_{i=1}^N\|v_i\|_2\,,
\|v\|_{2,\infty}:=\max_{i\in[N]}\|v_i\|_2.$
Finally,
$\mathbb S^{d-1}:=\{u\in\mathbb R^d:\|u\|_2=1\}$ and $\mathbb{B}_d$ denote the Euclidean unit
sphere and ball, $\Delta(\mathcal X)$ the set of probability distributions over
$\mathcal X$, and $\delta_x$ the Dirac measure at~$x$.\\
<!-- end head-1 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:cardinal-regret-noninvariant}
There exist ordinally equivalent smooth continuous games
$\mathcal G\sim_{\mathrm{ord}}\widetilde{\mathcal G}$ and a common sequence
of joint actions $(x_t)_{t\ge1}$ such that, for every even $T$,
$\operatorname{Reg}_{i,T}^{J}=0, \operatorname{Reg}_{i,T}^{\widetilde J}=\Omega(T).$
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:cce-not-ordinally-invar}
There exist ordinally equivalent continuous games
$\mathcal G\sim_{\mathrm{ord}}\widetilde{\mathcal G}$ and a distribution
$\mu\in\Delta(\mathcal X)$ such that
$\mu\in\mathrm{CCE}(\mathcal G),
\mu\notin\mathrm{CCE}(\widetilde{\mathcal G}).$
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:cce-vs-ne}
If $\mathcal{X}$ is compact, then $\bigcap_{\tilde{\mathcal{G}} \sim_{\mathrm{ord}} \mathcal{G}} \operatorname{CCE}(\tilde{\mathcal{G}}) = \Delta(\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal{G})).$
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:D-invariance}
For every smooth player-wise monotone reparameterization
$\widetilde J_i=\phi_i\circ J_i$ with $\phi_i'>0$,
$\widetilde D_i(x)=D_i(x)$ for all $i \in [N],x \in \mathcal{X}$.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:ordinal-regret}
The \emph{ordinal directional regret} of player $i \in [N]$ is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:def-directional-regret}
\operatorname{Reg}^{D}_{i,T}
:=
\sup_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i}
\sum_{t=1}^T
\left\langle
D_i(x_t),x_{i,t}-y_i
\right\rangle .
\end{equation}
We say that player $i$ has no ordinal directional regret if
$\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{\operatorname{Reg}^D_{i,T}}{T}\le0.$
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- assumption-1: body_md -->
\label{ass:block-pseudoconvex}
For every $i \in [N]$ and every $x_{-i} \in \mathcal{X}_{-i}$, the function
$z_i\longmapsto J_i(z_i,x_{-i})$ is pseudoconvex on $\mathcal X_i$, i.e., satisfies: 
For any $y_i \in \mathcal{X}_i, x = (x_i, x_{-i}) \in \mathcal{X}$,  
if $\left\langle \nabla_i J_i(x),y_i-x_i\right\rangle \geq 0$ 
then $J_i(y_i,x_{-i}) \geq J_i(x_i,x_{-i})$.
<!-- end assumption-1 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:ordinal-gap-ne}
Suppose the action sets are convex and
Assumption~\ref{ass:block-pseudoconvex} holds. Then
$\operatorname{Gap}_D(x)=0$ if and only if $x\in\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G).$
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
For $\mu\in\Delta(\mathcal X)$, define  
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:def-ordinal-coarse-var-gap}
\mathcal E_D(\mu)
:=
\sum_{i=1}^N
\left[
\sup_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i}
\mathbb E_{x\sim\mu}
\left\langle D_i(x),x_i-y_i\right\rangle
\right]_+ .
\end{equation}
We call $\mu$ an \emph{ordinal coarse variational equilibrium} if
$\mathcal E_D(\mu)=0$.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:discrete-regret}
Suppose $\eta_t=\eta>0$. Then, the algorithm~(\ref{eq:bnpgd-discrete}) incurs 
$\operatorname{Reg}^D_{i,T}
\leq \frac{R_i^2}{2\eta} +\frac{\eta T}{2}.$
With 
$\eta=R_i/\sqrt T$, 
$\operatorname{Reg}^D_{i,T} \leq R_i\sqrt T.$
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:discrete-equilibrium}
Let $\widehat\mu_T=T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^T\delta_{x_t}$. If every player runs
(\ref{eq:bnpgd-discrete}) with
$\eta_i=R_i/\sqrt T$, then
$\mathcal E_D(\widehat\mu_T)
\leq
\frac{1}{\sqrt T}
\sum_{i=1}^NR_i.$
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:comparison-field}
For any $i \in [N]$ and every $\delta>0$,
(i) $D_i^\delta$ is invariant under every strictly increasing transformation
$J_i\mapsto\phi_i\circ J_i$;
(ii) $\|D_i^\delta(x)\|\leq1$ for every $x\in\mathcal{X}^{\delta}$; 
(iii) for any $x \in \mathcal{X}$ with $x_i \in \operatorname{int}(\mathcal{X}_i)$ and $\nabla_iJ_i(x)\neq0$, $D_i^\delta(x)$ is well-defined for all sufficiently small $\delta > 0$, and 
$\lim_{\delta\downarrow0}D_i^\delta(x)=D_i(x)$.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:one-bit-estimator}
Let $\mathcal F_t = \sigma(\xi_0, \dots, \xi_{t-1}, U_0, \dots, U_{t-1})$ be the $\sigma$-algebra induced by the random uniform directions and comparison noise up to round $t-1$ for $t \geq 1$. In particular,
$z_t$ and
$x_t=\mathsf Q_{\delta_t}(z_t)$ are $\mathcal F_t$-measurable, and
$x_t\in\mathcal X^{\delta_t}$ by construction.
Conditionally on $\mathcal F_t$ and the current action profile
$x_t$,
$\mathbb E[\widehat D_{i,t}\mid\mathcal F_t]
= D_i^{\delta_t}(x_t),$
and $\mathbb E[\|\widehat D_{i,t}\|^2\mid\mathcal F_t] \leq G_i^2,$ 
where $G_i= \frac{1}{2\nu_i\kappa_{d_i}}
= \Theta\!\left(\frac{\sqrt{d_i}}{\nu_i}\right).$
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:comparison-regret}
Let player $i \in [N]$ run (\ref{eq:cngp}) with constant stepsize $\eta_i>0$ and radii
$0<\delta_t<\rho$. Then
$
\overline{\operatorname{Reg}}_{i,T}^{\delta}
\le
\frac{R_i^2}{2\eta_i}
+
\frac{\eta_iG_i^2T}{2}
+
\frac{R_i}{\rho_i}\sum_{t=1}^T\delta_t
$ for any $T \geq 1\,.$ If in addition  
$\eta_i=\frac{R_i}{G_i\sqrt T},
\delta_t=\frac{\delta}{\sqrt T},
0<\delta<\rho,$ 
then
\begin{equation}
\overline{\operatorname{Reg}}_{i,T}^{\delta}
\leq 
R_i(G_i+1)\sqrt T 
=
O\left(
\frac{R_i\sqrt{d_iT}}{\nu_i}
\right), \quad 
\mathbb E[\operatorname{Reg}_{i,T}^{\delta}]  
\leq 
3R_iG_i\sqrt T 
=
O\left(
\frac{R_i\sqrt{d_iT}}{\nu_i}
\right)\,.
\end{equation}
Furthermore, 
$\mathbb E\bigl[[\operatorname{Reg}_{i,T}^{\delta}]_+\bigr] \leq 4R_iG_i\sqrt T$.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- assumption-2: body_md -->
\label{as:non-degeneracy-ord-pot}
For every $i\in[N]$ and $x\in\mathcal X$,
$\nabla_i J_i(x)=0
\Longleftrightarrow
\nabla_i\Phi(x)=0.$
<!-- end assumption-2 -->
<!-- assumption-3: body_md -->
\label{as:rm-stepsizes} The stepsizes $(\eta_t)$ and the resolution $(\delta_t)$ satisfy: 
$\sum_{t=1}^\infty\eta_t=\infty, \sum_{t=1}^\infty\eta_t^2<\infty$ 
with $\eta_t>0, \delta_t \in (0,\rho), \forall t \in \mathbb{N}$ and $\delta_t\to0.$
<!-- end assumption-3 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:as-comparison-ne}
Let $\mathcal G$ be an ordinal potential game with ordinal potential $\Phi$, and
suppose that each $\mathcal X_i$ is compact and convex with nonempty interior. 
Assume that the costs $J_i$ and the potential $\Phi$ are continuously differentiable on an open neighborhood of $\mathcal{X}$ on which $\Phi$ is $L$-smooth.
Let Assumptions~\ref{ass:block-pseudoconvex} and
\ref{as:non-degeneracy-ord-pot} hold, and suppose that $\operatorname{int}_{\mathbb R}
\bigl(\Phi(\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G))\bigr)
=
\varnothing.$ 
Let $(x_t)$ be generated by (\ref{eq:cngp}), 
where $\widehat D_{i,t}$ is defined in
(\ref{eq:cngp}) under the gap-independent noisy
comparison model with $\nu_i\in(0,1/2]$.
Suppose that the stepsizes and comparison radii satisfy
Assumption~\ref{as:rm-stepsizes}. Then, with probability one,
$\operatorname{dist}
\bigl(x_t,\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)\bigr)
\longrightarrow 0$ and $\Phi(x_t)$ converges.  
If $\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)$ is finite, then there exists an
$\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)$-valued random variable $X_\infty$ such that
$x_t\to X_\infty$ almost surely. 
In particular, if the Nash equilibrium is unique, the iterates converge to it
almost surely.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
If $F_i(x)\neq0$, then
$
\frac{\widetilde F_i(x)}{\|\widetilde F_i(x)\|}
=
\frac{\phi_i'(J_i(x))F_i(x)}
     {\phi_i'(J_i(x))\|F_i(x)\|}
=
D_i(x),$
and $\widetilde F_i(x)=0$ if and only if $F_i(x)=0$.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Consider two players with $\mathcal X_i=\mathcal X_j=[0,1]$, and let player $i$ have the smooth cost
\[
J_i(x_i,x_j)=2-x_j+x_i(1-2x_j).
\]
The cost of the second player is immaterial. Suppose player $i$ always selects $x_{i,t}=1$, while player $j$ alternates between $x_{j,t}=0$ and $x_{j,t}=1$. Over every pair of rounds, player $i$ incurs total cost $3+0=3$. Any fixed comparator $y\in[0,1]$ incurs
\[
J_i(y,0)+J_i(y,1)=(2+y)+(1-y)=3.
\]
Hence $\operatorname{Reg}_{i,T}^{J}=0$ for every even $T$.

Now consider the ordinally equivalent representation $\widetilde J_i=\exp(J_i)$. The learner incurs $e^3+1$ over every pair of rounds, while a fixed comparator $y$ incurs $e^{2+y}+e^{1-y}$. Since $y\mapsto e^{2+y}+e^{1-y}$ is strictly increasing on $[0,1]$, its minimum is attained at $y=0$. Therefore
\[
\operatorname{Reg}_{i,T}^{\widetilde J}
=
\frac{T}{2}\left(e^3+1-e^2-e\right)
=
\Omega(T),
\]
while $\widetilde J_i=\exp(J_i)$ induces exactly the same preference comparisons as $J_i$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Consider a two-player game with $\mathcal X_1=\mathcal X_2=[0,1],$
and costs
\[
J_1(x_1,x_2)
=
2-x_2+x_1(1-2x_2),
\qquad
J_2(x_1,x_2)=0.
\]
Let $\mu =\frac12\delta_{(1,0)} + \frac12\delta_{(1,1)}.$ 
We first show that $\mu$ is a coarse correlated equilibrium of
$\mathcal G$. Under $\mu$, player~$1$ incurs expected cost
\[
\mathbb E_{x\sim\mu}[J_1(x)]
=
\frac12 J_1(1,0)+\frac12 J_1(1,1)
=
\frac12(3+0)
=
\frac32.
\]
For any fixed deviation $y_1\in[0,1]$,
\begin{equation*}
\mathbb E_{x\sim\mu}
\big[J_1(y_1,x_2)\big]
=
\frac12 J_1(y_1,0)
+
\frac12 J_1(y_1,1)
=
\frac12(2+y_1)
+
\frac12(1-y_1)
=
\frac32.
\end{equation*}
Thus player~$1$ cannot improve its expected cost by any fixed deviation.
Player~$2$ is indifferent between all actions since $J_2\equiv 0$.
Therefore $\mu\in\operatorname{CCE}(\mathcal G).$

Now define the ordinally equivalent game $\widetilde{\mathcal G}$ by
$\widetilde J_1=e^{J_1}, \widetilde J_2=J_2.$ 
Since $s\mapsto e^s$ is strictly increasing, the transformation preserves
all unilateral preference comparisons and best responses.
Under the same distribution $\mu$, player~$1$ now incurs expected cost
\[
\mathbb E_{x\sim\mu}[\widetilde J_1(x)]
=
\frac12 e^{J_1(1,0)}
+
\frac12 e^{J_1(1,1)}
=
\frac12(e^3+1).
\]
On the other hand, the fixed deviation $y_1=0$ yields
\[
\mathbb E_{x\sim\mu}
\big[\widetilde J_1(0,x_2)\big]
=
\frac12 e^{J_1(0,0)}
+
\frac12 e^{J_1(0,1)}
=
\frac12(e^2+e).
\]
Since
$e^3+1-e^2-e
= (e-1)^2(e+1)>0,$
we have
\[
\mathbb E_{x\sim\mu}[\widetilde J_1(x)]
>
\mathbb E_{x\sim\mu}
\big[\widetilde J_1(0,x_2)\big].
\]
Hence player~$1$ has a profitable fixed deviation, so
$\mu\notin\operatorname{CCE}(\widetilde{\mathcal G}).$
This proves that the CCE correspondence is not preserved under arbitrary
strictly increasing transformations.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- head-2: body_md -->
We start by highlighting the intuition behind the proof of the more challenging and interesting inclusion $\left( \bigcap_{\widetilde{\mathcal G}\in[\mathcal G]_{\rm ord}}
\operatorname{CCE}(\widetilde{\mathcal G})
\subseteq
\Delta(\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)) \right)$. 

For a fixed deviation $y_i$, ordinary CCE only requires

$$
\int q_{i,y_i}(z)\,\lambda(dz)\leq0,
\qquad
q_{i,y_i}(z)
=
\mathbb E[
J_i(X_i,z)\mid X_{-i}=z]
-
J_i(y_i,z).
$$

Hence positive conditional regret at some opponents' profiles may be offset
by negative conditional regret at others. Unilateral comparison feedback,
however, does not identify the relative cardinal scale of regret across
different $z$: replacing $J_i(x_i,z)$ by
$\alpha_i(z)J_i(x_i,z)$ for any positive $\alpha_i(z)$ leaves all
unilateral comparisons unchanged. Requiring the CCE inequality to hold
under every such reweighting eliminates cross-context cancellation and
forces

$$
q_{i,y_i}(z)\leq0
\qquad
\text{a.e.}
$$

for every deviation $y_i$. Taking the infimum over $y_i$ then gives

$$
\mathbb E[
J_i(X_i,z)\mid X_{-i}=z]
=
\min_{y_i}J_i(y_i,z),
$$

which is possible only if $X_i$ is a best response almost surely.
Thus robustness to all cardinal representations consistent with the
comparison oracle turns the global expected-regret inequalities defining CCE
into pointwise best-response conditions, yielding Nash support.


In what follows, we provide a complete and detailed proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:cce-vs-ne}. 

\begin{proof}
We prove the two inclusions separately.\\

\noindent\textbf{(i) Proof of $\Delta(\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G))
\subseteq \bigcap_{\widetilde{\mathcal G}\in[\mathcal G]_{\rm ord}}
\operatorname{CCE}(\widetilde{\mathcal G})$.}
Let $\mu\in\Delta(\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)),$ 
and consider an arbitrary
$\widetilde{\mathcal G}\in[\mathcal G]_{\rm ord}$.
By ordinal equivalence, for every $i \in [N]$, every $x_{-i} \in \mathcal{X}_{-i}$, and every
$a_i,b_i\in\mathcal X_i$,
\begin{equation*}
\operatorname{sgn}
\bigl(
J_i(a_i,x_{-i})-J_i(b_i,x_{-i})
\bigr)
=
\operatorname{sgn}
\bigl(
\widetilde J_i(a_i,x_{-i})
-
\widetilde J_i(b_i,x_{-i})
\bigr).    
\end{equation*}
Hence the two cost functions induce exactly the same minimizers on every
unilateral slice:
$\operatorname{BR}^{\mathcal G}_i(x_{-i})=
\operatorname{BR}^{\widetilde{\mathcal G}}_i(x_{-i}).$ Therefore, 
$\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G) = \operatorname{NE}(\widetilde{\mathcal G}).$

Since $\mu$ is supported on $\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)$, for every
player $i \in [N]$ and every fixed deviation $y_i\in\mathcal X_i$,
$\widetilde J_i(x_i,x_{-i})\leq \widetilde J_i(y_i,x_{-i})$ 
for $\mu$-a.e. $x$. 
Integrating gives $\mathbb E_{x\sim\mu} \bigl[\widetilde J_i(x)\bigr] \leq \mathbb E_{x\sim\mu} \bigl[\widetilde J_i(y_i,x_{-i})\bigr].$ 
Thus $\mu\in\operatorname{CCE}(\widetilde{\mathcal G}).$
Since $\widetilde{\mathcal G}\in[\mathcal G]_{\rm ord}$ was arbitrary,
$\mu\in\bigcap_{\widetilde{\mathcal G}\in[\mathcal G]_{\rm ord}}
\operatorname{CCE}(\widetilde{\mathcal G}).$\\

\noindent\textbf{(ii) Proof of
$\displaystyle
\bigcap_{\widetilde{\mathcal G}\in[\mathcal G]_{\rm ord}}
\operatorname{CCE}(\widetilde{\mathcal G})
\subseteq
\Delta(\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G))$.}
Let $\mu\in\bigcap_{\widetilde{\mathcal G}\in[\mathcal G]_{\rm ord}}
\operatorname{CCE}(\widetilde{\mathcal G}).$ 

We show that $\mu$ is supported on $\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)$.

\emph{Proof strategy.}
Fix a player $i \in [N]$. Conditional on an opponents' profile $z=x_{-i} \in \mathcal{X}_{-i}$, let

$$
A_i(z)
:=
\mathbb E_\mu\!\left[J_i(X_i,z)\mid X_{-i}=z\right]
$$

denote player $i$'s conditional expected cost. For a fixed deviation
$y_i\in\mathcal X_i$, define its conditional regret for any $z \in \mathcal{X}_{-i}$ as follows: 
$$
q_{i,y_i}(z)
:=
A_i(z)-J_i(y_i,z).
$$

The key step is to show that ordinal robustness forces
$$
q_{i,y_i}(z)\leq0
\qquad
\text{for a.e. }z
$$

for every fixed $y_i$. Otherwise, an ordinally invisible positive rescaling
of costs could amplify the contexts in which $q_{i,y_i}>0$ and violate the
CCE inequality. We then remove the dependence on $y_i$ of the exceptional null set 
using a countable dense set of deviations. This yields
$$
A_i(z)
\leq
\min_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i}J_i(y_i,z)
\qquad\text{a.e.}
$$
The reverse inequality holds automatically because $A_i(z)$ is an average
of the values $J_i(\cdot,z)$. Hence the conditional average equals the
minimum, which is possible only if the conditional distribution of $X_i$
is supported on best responses. Repeating this argument for every player
implies $\mu(\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G))=1$.

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Step 1: Ordinal robustness implies nonpositive conditional regret.}
Fix $i\in[N]$, write $z:=x_{-i}$. 
Denote by $\pi_{-i}:\mathcal X\to\mathcal X_{-i},\pi_{-i}(x)=x_{-i},$
the canonical projection onto the opponents' action space. Let
$\lambda:=\mu_{-i}:=(\pi_{-i})_{\#}\mu$ be the marginal distribution of $X_{-i}$ induced by $\mu$; equivalently, for every Borel set $B\subseteq\mathcal X_{-i}$,
$\mu_{-i}(B) = \mu\bigl(\mathcal X_i\times B\bigr).$ 
Since $\mathcal X_i$ and $\mathcal X_{-i}$ are compact metric spaces, there exists a regular conditional distribution
$\kappa_i(\,\cdot\mid z)$ of $X_i$ given $X_{-i}=z$ such that the disintegration
$\mu(dx_i,dz) =\kappa_i(dx_i\mid z)\,\mu_{-i}(dz) = \kappa_i(dx_i\mid z) \lambda(dz)$ holds.

Define for any $z \in \mathcal{X}_{-i}$ the average cost of player $i \in [N]$ as follows: 
$$
A_i(z)
:=
\int_{\mathcal X_i}
J_i(x_i,z)\,
\kappa_i(dx_i|z).
$$
Since $J_i$ is continuous on the compact set $\mathcal X$, it is bounded,
and therefore $A_i$ is bounded and measurable. Fix an arbitrary deviation $y_i\in\mathcal X_i$ and define 
the conditional regret
$$
q_{i,y_i}(z)
:=
A_i(z)-J_i(y_i,z).
$$

We claim that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:conditional-regret-nonpositive}
q_{i,y_i}(z)\leq0
\qquad
\text{for $\lambda$-a.e. }z.
\end{equation}

To see this, let $h$ be any nonnegative smooth function defined on an open
neighborhood of $\mathcal X_{-i}$, and let $t\geq0$. Consider the game
$\mathcal G^{t,h}$ obtained by replacing player $i$'s cost by
$$
J_i^{t,h}(x_i,z)
:=
\bigl(1+t h(z)\bigr)J_i(x_i,z)\,, \quad \forall x_i \in \mathcal{X}_i, \forall z \in \mathcal{X}_{-i}\,,
$$
and leaving every other player's cost unchanged. Since
$1+t h(z)>0,$ this transformation preserves every unilateral comparison: for any $a_i, b_i \in \mathcal{X}_i, z \in \mathcal{X}_{-i},$
$$
\operatorname{sgn}\!\left(
J_i^{t,h}(a_i,z)-J_i^{t,h}(b_i,z)
\right)
=
\operatorname{sgn}\!\left(
J_i(a_i,z)-J_i(b_i,z)
\right).
$$
Therefore, $\mathcal G^{t,h}\sim_{\rm ord}\mathcal G.$
Since $\mu\in\bigcap_{\widetilde{\mathcal G}\in[\mathcal G]_{\rm ord}}
\operatorname{CCE}(\widetilde{\mathcal G}),$  it follows that 
$\mu\in\operatorname{CCE}(\mathcal G^{t,h}).$ Then by definition of $\operatorname{CCE}(\mathcal G^{t,h})$, we have 
for the fixed deviation $y_i \in \mathcal{X}_i$, 
$$
\mathbb E_\mu\!\left[
\bigl(1+t h(X_{-i})\bigr)
\bigl(
J_i(X_i,X_{-i})-J_i(y_i,X_{-i})
\bigr)
\right]
\leq0.
$$

Disintegrating with respect to $X_{-i}$ (recall that $\mu(dx_i,dz) = \kappa_i(dx_i |z) \lambda(dz)$) yields: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:weighted-regret}
\int_{\mathcal X_{-i}}
\bigl(1+t h(z)\bigr)
q_{i,y_i}(z)
\lambda(dz)
\leq0\,, 
\qquad
\forall t\geq0.
\end{equation}
Therefore, 
$$
\int_{\mathcal X_{-i}} h(z) q_{i,y_i}(z)\,\lambda(dz)
\leq - \frac{1}{t} \int_{\mathcal X_{-i}} q_{i,y_i}(z)\,\lambda(dz) \,, 
\qquad
\forall t > 0.
$$
Taking the limit $t \to + \infty$ implies that necessarily: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:test-function}
\int_{\mathcal X_{-i}}
h(z)q_{i,y_i}(z) 
\lambda(dz)
\leq0
\end{equation}
for every nonnegative smooth $h$. 

We now localize (\ref{eq:test-function}), i.e., we use the freedom to choose the test function $h$ so that it concentrates on an arbitrary region of $\mathcal{X}_{-i}$, to pass from the global integral inequality~\ref{eq:test-function} to the local/almost-everywhere statement~(\ref{eq:conditional-regret-nonpositive}) by using test functions with localized support. Suppose, toward a contradiction,
that
$$
\lambda\bigl(\{z \in \mathcal{X}_{-i}:q_{i,y_i}(z)>0\}\bigr)>0.
$$
Then, for some $\varepsilon>0$,
$E_\varepsilon:=\{z:q_{i,y_i}(z)\geq\varepsilon\}$ 
has positive $\lambda$-measure. By inner regularity of $\lambda$, choose a
compact set
$K\subseteq E_\varepsilon,\, \lambda(K)>0.$

Since $q_{i,y_i}$ is bounded by boundedness of $J_i$ over the compact set $\mathcal{X}$, let $M:=\|q_{i,y_i}\|_\infty < \infty.$ By outer regularity, choose an open neighborhood $U\supseteq K$ such that
$$
\lambda(U\setminus K)
<
\frac{\varepsilon\lambda(K)}{2M},
$$
where the claim is immediate if $M=0$. Choose a smooth bump function $h$
satisfying: 
$$
0\leq h\leq1,
\qquad
h=1\ \text{on }K,
\qquad
\operatorname{supp}(h)\cap\mathcal X_{-i}\subseteq U.
$$
Then
$$
\begin{aligned}
\int h(z)q_{i,y_i}(z)\,\lambda(dz)
&\geq
\int_K q_{i,y_i}(z)\,\lambda(dz)
-
\int_{U\setminus K}|q_{i,y_i}(z)|\,\lambda(dz)
\\
&\geq
\varepsilon\lambda(K)-M\lambda(U\setminus K)
\\
&>
\frac{\varepsilon\lambda(K)}{2}
>0,
\end{aligned}
$$

contradicting (\ref{eq:test-function}). Hence
$q_{i,y_i}(z)\leq0, 
\lambda\text{-a.e.}$
for every fixed $y_i\in\mathcal X_i$. Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:conditional-fixed-y}
A_i(z)
\leq
J_i(y_i,z)\,,
\qquad
\lambda\text{-a.e.}
\end{equation}

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Step 2: Obtain a common full-measure set for all deviations.}
The exceptional null set in (\ref{eq:conditional-fixed-y}) may depend on
$y_i$. To remove this dependence, let
$D_i\subseteq\mathcal X_i$
be a countable dense subset. For each $y_i\in D_i$, let $Z_{i,y_i}$ be a
set of full $\lambda$-measure on which
$A_i(z)\leq J_i(y_i,z).$ 
Because $D_i$ is countable,
$Z_i:=\bigcap_{y_i\in D_i} Z_{i,y_i}$
still satisfies
$\lambda(Z_i)=1.$
Thus, for every $z\in Z_i$,
$$
A_i(z)\leq J_i(y_i,z)
\qquad
\forall y_i\in D_i.
$$
By continuity of $J_i(\cdot,z)$ and density of $D_i$,
$$
A_i(z)
\leq
\inf_{y_i\in D_i}J_i(y_i,z)
=
\min_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i}J_i(y_i,z)\,,
\qquad
\forall z\in Z_i.
$$
We have therefore shown that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:A-less-than-min}
A_i(z)\leq \min_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i}J_i(y_i,z)
\qquad
\lambda\text{-a.e.}
\end{equation}

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Step 3: Nonpositive conditional regret forces best-response play.}
Note first that for any $z \in \mathcal{X}_{-i}$, 
$$
J_i(x_i,z)\geq \min_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i}J_i(y_i,z)\,, 
\qquad
\forall x_i\in\mathcal X_i.
$$

Averaging with respect to $\kappa_i(\cdot|z)$ gives 
$$
A_i(z)
=
\int_{\mathcal X_i}J_i(x_i,z)\,
\kappa_i(dx_i\mid z)
\geq
\min_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i}J_i(y_i,z).
$$

Together with (\ref{eq:A-less-than-min}), this yields
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:average-equals-min}
A_i(z)=\min_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i}J_i(y_i,z)
\qquad
\lambda\text{-a.e.}
\end{equation}

Now define for any $x_i \in \mathcal{X}_i, x_{-i} \in \mathcal{X}_{-i}$, 
$r_i(x_i,z) := J_i(x_i,z)-\min_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i}J_i(y_i,z)\geq0.$

For $\lambda$-a.e. $z$, (\ref{eq:average-equals-min}) implies
$$
\int_{\mathcal X_i}
r_i(x_i,z)\,
\kappa_i(dx_i\mid z)
=
A_i(z)-\min_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i}J_i(y_i,z)
=
0.
$$

Since $r_i(\cdot,z)$ is nonnegative, it must vanish
$\kappa_i(\cdot\mid z)$-almost surely. Therefore

$$
\kappa_i
\bigl(
\operatorname{BR}^{\mathcal G}_i(z)
\mid z
\bigr)
=
1
\qquad
\lambda\text{-a.e. }z.
$$
Integrating over $z$ gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:player-best-response-as}
\mu\left(
\left\{
x \in \mathcal{X}:
x_i\in\operatorname{BR}_i^{\mathcal G}(x_{-i})
\right\}
\right) = 1\,.
\end{equation}

\noindent\emph{Step 4: Intersect the best-response conditions across players.}
Since (\ref{eq:player-best-response-as}) holds for every $i\in[N]$, and
the number of players is finite,

$$
\mu\!\left(
\bigcap_{i=1}^N
\left\{
x \in \mathcal{X}:
x_i\in\operatorname{BR}_i^{\mathcal G}(x_{-i})
\right\}
\right)
=
1.
$$

The set inside the probability is exactly
$\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)$. Hence
$\mu(\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G))=1,$
or equivalently,
$\mu\in\Delta(\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)),$
which concludes the proof of the desired inclusion. 
\end{proof}

<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
We prove the two inclusions separately.\\

\noindent\textbf{(i) Proof of $\Delta(\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G))
\subseteq \bigcap_{\widetilde{\mathcal G}\in[\mathcal G]_{\rm ord}}
\operatorname{CCE}(\widetilde{\mathcal G})$.}
Let $\mu\in\Delta(\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)),$ 
and consider an arbitrary
$\widetilde{\mathcal G}\in[\mathcal G]_{\rm ord}$.
By ordinal equivalence, for every $i \in [N]$, every $x_{-i} \in \mathcal{X}_{-i}$, and every
$a_i,b_i\in\mathcal X_i$,
\begin{equation*}
\operatorname{sgn}
\bigl(
J_i(a_i,x_{-i})-J_i(b_i,x_{-i})
\bigr)
=
\operatorname{sgn}
\bigl(
\widetilde J_i(a_i,x_{-i})
-
\widetilde J_i(b_i,x_{-i})
\bigr).    
\end{equation*}
Hence the two cost functions induce exactly the same minimizers on every
unilateral slice:
$\operatorname{BR}^{\mathcal G}_i(x_{-i})=
\operatorname{BR}^{\widetilde{\mathcal G}}_i(x_{-i}).$ Therefore, 
$\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G) = \operatorname{NE}(\widetilde{\mathcal G}).$

Since $\mu$ is supported on $\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)$, for every
player $i \in [N]$ and every fixed deviation $y_i\in\mathcal X_i$,
$\widetilde J_i(x_i,x_{-i})\leq \widetilde J_i(y_i,x_{-i})$ 
for $\mu$-a.e. $x$. 
Integrating gives $\mathbb E_{x\sim\mu} \bigl[\widetilde J_i(x)\bigr] \leq \mathbb E_{x\sim\mu} \bigl[\widetilde J_i(y_i,x_{-i})\bigr].$ 
Thus $\mu\in\operatorname{CCE}(\widetilde{\mathcal G}).$
Since $\widetilde{\mathcal G}\in[\mathcal G]_{\rm ord}$ was arbitrary,
$\mu\in\bigcap_{\widetilde{\mathcal G}\in[\mathcal G]_{\rm ord}}
\operatorname{CCE}(\widetilde{\mathcal G}).$\\

\noindent\textbf{(ii) Proof of
$\displaystyle
\bigcap_{\widetilde{\mathcal G}\in[\mathcal G]_{\rm ord}}
\operatorname{CCE}(\widetilde{\mathcal G})
\subseteq
\Delta(\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G))$.}
Let $\mu\in\bigcap_{\widetilde{\mathcal G}\in[\mathcal G]_{\rm ord}}
\operatorname{CCE}(\widetilde{\mathcal G}).$ 

We show that $\mu$ is supported on $\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)$.

\emph{Proof strategy.}
Fix a player $i \in [N]$. Conditional on an opponents' profile $z=x_{-i} \in \mathcal{X}_{-i}$, let

$$
A_i(z)
:=
\mathbb E_\mu\!\left[J_i(X_i,z)\mid X_{-i}=z\right]
$$

denote player $i$'s conditional expected cost. For a fixed deviation
$y_i\in\mathcal X_i$, define its conditional regret for any $z \in \mathcal{X}_{-i}$ as follows: 
$$
q_{i,y_i}(z)
:=
A_i(z)-J_i(y_i,z).
$$

The key step is to show that ordinal robustness forces
$$
q_{i,y_i}(z)\leq0
\qquad
\text{for a.e. }z
$$

for every fixed $y_i$. Otherwise, an ordinally invisible positive rescaling
of costs could amplify the contexts in which $q_{i,y_i}>0$ and violate the
CCE inequality. We then remove the dependence on $y_i$ of the exceptional null set 
using a countable dense set of deviations. This yields
$$
A_i(z)
\leq
\min_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i}J_i(y_i,z)
\qquad\text{a.e.}
$$
The reverse inequality holds automatically because $A_i(z)$ is an average
of the values $J_i(\cdot,z)$. Hence the conditional average equals the
minimum, which is possible only if the conditional distribution of $X_i$
is supported on best responses. Repeating this argument for every player
implies $\mu(\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G))=1$.

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Step 1: Ordinal robustness implies nonpositive conditional regret.}
Fix $i\in[N]$, write $z:=x_{-i}$. 
Denote by $\pi_{-i}:\mathcal X\to\mathcal X_{-i},\pi_{-i}(x)=x_{-i},$
the canonical projection onto the opponents' action space. Let
$\lambda:=\mu_{-i}:=(\pi_{-i})_{\#}\mu$ be the marginal distribution of $X_{-i}$ induced by $\mu$; equivalently, for every Borel set $B\subseteq\mathcal X_{-i}$,
$\mu_{-i}(B) = \mu\bigl(\mathcal X_i\times B\bigr).$ 
Since $\mathcal X_i$ and $\mathcal X_{-i}$ are compact metric spaces, there exists a regular conditional distribution
$\kappa_i(\,\cdot\mid z)$ of $X_i$ given $X_{-i}=z$ such that the disintegration
$\mu(dx_i,dz) =\kappa_i(dx_i\mid z)\,\mu_{-i}(dz) = \kappa_i(dx_i\mid z) \lambda(dz)$ holds.

Define for any $z \in \mathcal{X}_{-i}$ the average cost of player $i \in [N]$ as follows: 
$$
A_i(z)
:=
\int_{\mathcal X_i}
J_i(x_i,z)\,
\kappa_i(dx_i|z).
$$
Since $J_i$ is continuous on the compact set $\mathcal X$, it is bounded,
and therefore $A_i$ is bounded and measurable. Fix an arbitrary deviation $y_i\in\mathcal X_i$ and define 
the conditional regret
$$
q_{i,y_i}(z)
:=
A_i(z)-J_i(y_i,z).
$$

We claim that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:conditional-regret-nonpositive}
q_{i,y_i}(z)\leq0
\qquad
\text{for $\lambda$-a.e. }z.
\end{equation}

To see this, let $h$ be any nonnegative smooth function defined on an open
neighborhood of $\mathcal X_{-i}$, and let $t\geq0$. Consider the game
$\mathcal G^{t,h}$ obtained by replacing player $i$'s cost by
$$
J_i^{t,h}(x_i,z)
:=
\bigl(1+t h(z)\bigr)J_i(x_i,z)\,, \quad \forall x_i \in \mathcal{X}_i, \forall z \in \mathcal{X}_{-i}\,,
$$
and leaving every other player's cost unchanged. Since
$1+t h(z)>0,$ this transformation preserves every unilateral comparison: for any $a_i, b_i \in \mathcal{X}_i, z \in \mathcal{X}_{-i},$
$$
\operatorname{sgn}\!\left(
J_i^{t,h}(a_i,z)-J_i^{t,h}(b_i,z)
\right)
=
\operatorname{sgn}\!\left(
J_i(a_i,z)-J_i(b_i,z)
\right).
$$
Therefore, $\mathcal G^{t,h}\sim_{\rm ord}\mathcal G.$
Since $\mu\in\bigcap_{\widetilde{\mathcal G}\in[\mathcal G]_{\rm ord}}
\operatorname{CCE}(\widetilde{\mathcal G}),$  it follows that 
$\mu\in\operatorname{CCE}(\mathcal G^{t,h}).$ Then by definition of $\operatorname{CCE}(\mathcal G^{t,h})$, we have 
for the fixed deviation $y_i \in \mathcal{X}_i$, 
$$
\mathbb E_\mu\!\left[
\bigl(1+t h(X_{-i})\bigr)
\bigl(
J_i(X_i,X_{-i})-J_i(y_i,X_{-i})
\bigr)
\right]
\leq0.
$$

Disintegrating with respect to $X_{-i}$ (recall that $\mu(dx_i,dz) = \kappa_i(dx_i |z) \lambda(dz)$) yields: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:weighted-regret}
\int_{\mathcal X_{-i}}
\bigl(1+t h(z)\bigr)
q_{i,y_i}(z)
\lambda(dz)
\leq0\,, 
\qquad
\forall t\geq0.
\end{equation}
Therefore, 
$$
\int_{\mathcal X_{-i}} h(z) q_{i,y_i}(z)\,\lambda(dz)
\leq - \frac{1}{t} \int_{\mathcal X_{-i}} q_{i,y_i}(z)\,\lambda(dz) \,, 
\qquad
\forall t > 0.
$$
Taking the limit $t \to + \infty$ implies that necessarily: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:test-function}
\int_{\mathcal X_{-i}}
h(z)q_{i,y_i}(z) 
\lambda(dz)
\leq0
\end{equation}
for every nonnegative smooth $h$. 

We now localize (\ref{eq:test-function}), i.e., we use the freedom to choose the test function $h$ so that it concentrates on an arbitrary region of $\mathcal{X}_{-i}$, to pass from the global integral inequality~\ref{eq:test-function} to the local/almost-everywhere statement~(\ref{eq:conditional-regret-nonpositive}) by using test functions with localized support. Suppose, toward a contradiction,
that
$$
\lambda\bigl(\{z \in \mathcal{X}_{-i}:q_{i,y_i}(z)>0\}\bigr)>0.
$$
Then, for some $\varepsilon>0$,
$E_\varepsilon:=\{z:q_{i,y_i}(z)\geq\varepsilon\}$ 
has positive $\lambda$-measure. By inner regularity of $\lambda$, choose a
compact set
$K\subseteq E_\varepsilon,\, \lambda(K)>0.$

Since $q_{i,y_i}$ is bounded by boundedness of $J_i$ over the compact set $\mathcal{X}$, let $M:=\|q_{i,y_i}\|_\infty < \infty.$ By outer regularity, choose an open neighborhood $U\supseteq K$ such that
$$
\lambda(U\setminus K)
<
\frac{\varepsilon\lambda(K)}{2M},
$$
where the claim is immediate if $M=0$. Choose a smooth bump function $h$
satisfying: 
$$
0\leq h\leq1,
\qquad
h=1\ \text{on }K,
\qquad
\operatorname{supp}(h)\cap\mathcal X_{-i}\subseteq U.
$$
Then
$$
\begin{aligned}
\int h(z)q_{i,y_i}(z)\,\lambda(dz)
&\geq
\int_K q_{i,y_i}(z)\,\lambda(dz)
-
\int_{U\setminus K}|q_{i,y_i}(z)|\,\lambda(dz)
\\
&\geq
\varepsilon\lambda(K)-M\lambda(U\setminus K)
\\
&>
\frac{\varepsilon\lambda(K)}{2}
>0,
\end{aligned}
$$

contradicting (\ref{eq:test-function}). Hence
$q_{i,y_i}(z)\leq0, 
\lambda\text{-a.e.}$
for every fixed $y_i\in\mathcal X_i$. Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:conditional-fixed-y}
A_i(z)
\leq
J_i(y_i,z)\,,
\qquad
\lambda\text{-a.e.}
\end{equation}

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Step 2: Obtain a common full-measure set for all deviations.}
The exceptional null set in (\ref{eq:conditional-fixed-y}) may depend on
$y_i$. To remove this dependence, let
$D_i\subseteq\mathcal X_i$
be a countable dense subset. For each $y_i\in D_i$, let $Z_{i,y_i}$ be a
set of full $\lambda$-measure on which
$A_i(z)\leq J_i(y_i,z).$ 
Because $D_i$ is countable,
$Z_i:=\bigcap_{y_i\in D_i} Z_{i,y_i}$
still satisfies
$\lambda(Z_i)=1.$
Thus, for every $z\in Z_i$,
$$
A_i(z)\leq J_i(y_i,z)
\qquad
\forall y_i\in D_i.
$$
By continuity of $J_i(\cdot,z)$ and density of $D_i$,
$$
A_i(z)
\leq
\inf_{y_i\in D_i}J_i(y_i,z)
=
\min_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i}J_i(y_i,z)\,,
\qquad
\forall z\in Z_i.
$$
We have therefore shown that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:A-less-than-min}
A_i(z)\leq \min_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i}J_i(y_i,z)
\qquad
\lambda\text{-a.e.}
\end{equation}

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Step 3: Nonpositive conditional regret forces best-response play.}
Note first that for any $z \in \mathcal{X}_{-i}$, 
$$
J_i(x_i,z)\geq \min_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i}J_i(y_i,z)\,, 
\qquad
\forall x_i\in\mathcal X_i.
$$

Averaging with respect to $\kappa_i(\cdot|z)$ gives 
$$
A_i(z)
=
\int_{\mathcal X_i}J_i(x_i,z)\,
\kappa_i(dx_i\mid z)
\geq
\min_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i}J_i(y_i,z).
$$

Together with (\ref{eq:A-less-than-min}), this yields
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:average-equals-min}
A_i(z)=\min_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i}J_i(y_i,z)
\qquad
\lambda\text{-a.e.}
\end{equation}

Now define for any $x_i \in \mathcal{X}_i, x_{-i} \in \mathcal{X}_{-i}$, 
$r_i(x_i,z) := J_i(x_i,z)-\min_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i}J_i(y_i,z)\geq0.$

For $\lambda$-a.e. $z$, (\ref{eq:average-equals-min}) implies
$$
\int_{\mathcal X_i}
r_i(x_i,z)\,
\kappa_i(dx_i\mid z)
=
A_i(z)-\min_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i}J_i(y_i,z)
=
0.
$$

Since $r_i(\cdot,z)$ is nonnegative, it must vanish
$\kappa_i(\cdot\mid z)$-almost surely. Therefore

$$
\kappa_i
\bigl(
\operatorname{BR}^{\mathcal G}_i(z)
\mid z
\bigr)
=
1
\qquad
\lambda\text{-a.e. }z.
$$
Integrating over $z$ gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:player-best-response-as}
\mu\left(
\left\{
x \in \mathcal{X}:
x_i\in\operatorname{BR}_i^{\mathcal G}(x_{-i})
\right\}
\right) = 1\,.
\end{equation}

\noindent\emph{Step 4: Intersect the best-response conditions across players.}
Since (\ref{eq:player-best-response-as}) holds for every $i\in[N]$, and
the number of players is finite,

$$
\mu\!\left(
\bigcap_{i=1}^N
\left\{
x \in \mathcal{X}:
x_i\in\operatorname{BR}_i^{\mathcal G}(x_{-i})
\right\}
\right)
=
1.
$$

The set inside the probability is exactly
$\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)$. Hence
$\mu(\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G))=1,$
or equivalently,
$\mu\in\Delta(\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)),$
which concludes the proof of the desired inclusion.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Since every summand in (\ref{eq:ordinal-nash-gap}) is nonnegative, $\operatorname{Gap}_D(x)=0$ if and only if, 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:variational-charac}
\left\langle D_i(x),y_i-x_i\right\rangle \geq 0\, \quad \forall i \in [N], \forall\, y_i \in \mathcal{X}_i.
\end{equation}
If $\nabla_iJ_i(x)\neq0$, multiplying the previous inequality~(\ref{eq:variational-charac}) by the positive scalar $\|\nabla_iJ_i(x)\|$ yields $\langle\nabla_iJ_i(x),y_i-x_i\rangle\geq0$ for all $y_i$. If $\nabla_iJ_i(x)=0$, the same condition holds trivially. 

By pseudoconvexity, this first-order condition is sufficient for $x_i$ to globally minimize $J_i(\cdot,x_{-i})$ and hence $x$ is a Nash equilibrium. 

Conversely, if $x$ is a NE, since every global minimizer of a differentiable function over a convex set satisfies the corresponding first-order variational inequality, it follows that (\ref{eq:variational-charac}) holds and hence by the first step in this proof we obtain that $\operatorname{Gap}_D(x)=0$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:dynamics-invariant}
Suppose $\widetilde J_i=\phi_i\circ J_i$ for every $i$, where
$\phi_i'>0$. Then the block-normalized projected vector fields generated
by $\mathcal G = ((\mathcal{X})_{i \in [N]}, (J_i)_{i \in [N]})$ and $\widetilde{\mathcal G} = ((\mathcal{X})_{i \in [N]}, (\tilde{J}_i)_{i \in [N]})$ coincide.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:ct-regret}
Every trajectory of (\ref{eq:BNPGD}) satisfies
$\operatorname{Reg}_i^D(T)\leq\frac{\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal X_i)^2}{2}$ for every $T\geq 0.$
Consequently,
$\frac{\operatorname{Reg}_i^D(T)}{T}=O(T^{-1}).$
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:ct-ocve}
The occupation measure of block-normalized play satisfies
$\mathcal E_D(\mu_T)
\leq
\frac{1}{2T}\sum_{i=1}^N R_i^2.$
Hence time-average play converges to the set of ordinal coarse variational
equilibria at rate $O(T^{-1})$.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:ordinal-potential-gradient-alignment}
Suppose that $\mathcal G$ is an ordinal potential game with costs
$J_i$ and ordinal potential $\Phi$ that are $C^1$ on an open
neighborhood of $\mathcal X$, and suppose that each $\mathcal X_i$ has nonempty interior. 
Under Assumption~\ref{as:non-degeneracy-ord-pot}, for every
$i\in[N]$ and every $x\in\mathcal X$, either
$\nabla_iJ_i(x) = \nabla_i\Phi(x) = 0$, 
or there exists a scalar $\lambda_i(x)>0$ s.t. 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:positive-gradient-collinearity}
\nabla_iJ_i(x)
=
\lambda_i(x)\nabla_i\Phi(x).
\end{equation}
In particular, whenever the gradients are nonzero,
$
D_i(x)= \frac{\nabla_iJ_i(x)}
{\|\nabla_iJ_i(x)\|}
=
\frac{\nabla_i\Phi(x)}
{\|\nabla_i\Phi(x)\|}.
$
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
The nondegeneracy condition is needed because ordinal equivalence alone
does not preserve first-order stationarity. For example, the functions
$s\mapsto s$ and $s\mapsto s^3$ induce the same ordering on
$\mathbb R$, although their derivatives behave differently at the
origin. Thus, without nondegeneracy, an ordinal representation could
create or remove stationary blocks. Assumption~\ref{as:non-degeneracy-ord-pot}
rules out precisely this pathology and allows first-order stationarity
of the potential to be identified with first-order stationarity of the
players' costs.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:cv-BNPG-ordinal-pot}
Let $\mathcal{G}$ be an ordinal potential game with potential $\Phi$ such that
Assumption~\ref{as:non-degeneracy-ord-pot} holds. 
Suppose in addition that $\mathcal{X}_i$ has nonempty interior for any $i \in [N]$.  
Then the following statements
hold:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*), leftmargin=*,itemsep=0pt,topsep=0pt]
\item \label{pot-dissipation} (potential dissipation)
$\frac{d}{dt}\Phi(x(t))
= - \sum_{i=1}^N \|\nabla_i\Phi(x(t))\|
\,
\|\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}\!\left(-D_i(x)\right)\|^2
\le 0$ for almost every $t$ along every absolutely
continuous solution of (\ref{eq:BNPGD}).
\item \label{pot-dissipation-interior} (block-$\ell_{2,1}$ dissipation)
$\frac{d}{dt}\Phi(x(t)) =
-\sum_{i=1}^N \|\nabla_i\Phi(x(t))\|
= -\|\nabla\Phi(x(t))\|_{2,1}$
for almost every $t$, if the trajectory remains in $\operatorname{int}(\mathcal X)$.
\item \label{fos} (first-order stationarity)
For any $T>0$,
$\min_{0\le t\le T}
\|\nabla\Phi(x(t))\|_{2,1}
\le
\frac{\Phi(x(0))-\Phi_{\inf}}{T},$
where
$\Phi_{\inf}:=\inf_{x\in\mathcal X}\Phi(x)>-\infty$,
if again the trajectory remains interior.
\item \label{convergence} (convergence)
Assuming a solution of (\ref{eq:BNPGD}) remains in a compact set
$K\subset\operatorname{int}(\mathcal X)$ and $\nabla\Phi$ is Lipschitz on $K$,
then for every $i\in[N]$,
$\|\nabla_i\Phi(x(t))\|\longrightarrow0.$
If in addition Assumption~\ref{ass:block-pseudoconvex} holds, then the rest
points of (\ref{eq:BNPGD}) coincide exactly with the set of Nash equilibria
and
$\operatorname{dist}\bigl(x(t),\operatorname{NE}\bigr)\longrightarrow 0.$
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Fix $y_i\in\mathcal X_i$ and define $V_i(t)=\frac12\|x_i(t)-y_i\|^2$. The characterization of the projection in the  (\ref{eq:BNPGD}) dynamics yields $-D_i(x)=\dot x_i+n_i$ for some $n_i\in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)$, where $N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)$ denotes the normal cone to the set $\mathcal{X}_i$. Since $\langle n_i,y_i-x_i\rangle\leq0$ by definition of the normal cone,
\[
\dot V_i(t)=\langle \dot x_i, x_i-y_i\rangle
= -\langle D_i(x),x_i-y_i\rangle + \langle n_i,y_i-x_i\rangle
\leq-\langle D_i(x),x_i-y_i\rangle.
\]
Integrating the above inequality from $0$ to $T$ and rearranging gives: 
\[
\int_0^T\langle D_i(x(t)),x_i(t)-y_i\rangle\,dt
\leq
\frac12\|x_i(0)-y_i\|^2
-\frac12\|x_i(T)-y_i\|^2
\leq\frac{\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal X_i)^2}{2}.
\]
Taking the supremum over $y_i$ proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Let $i\in[N]$ and $x\in\mathcal X$.
By Assumption~\ref{as:non-degeneracy-ord-pot},
the two block gradients vanish simultaneously. Thus it remains only
to consider the case $\nabla_iJ_i(x)\neq0, \nabla_i\Phi(x)\neq0\,.$

Suppose first that $x_i\in\operatorname{int}(\mathcal X_i)$.
Define the two $C^1$ functions
\[
f_i(y_i):=J_i(y_i,x_{-i}),
\qquad
\phi_i(y_i):=\Phi(y_i,x_{-i}).
\]
The ordinal-potential property implies that, for all
$y_i,z_i\in\mathcal X_i$,
\[
\operatorname{sgn}
\bigl(
f_i(y_i)-f_i(z_i)
\bigr)
=
\operatorname{sgn}
\bigl(
\phi_i(y_i)-\phi_i(z_i)
\bigr).
\]
We claim that the two gradients $\nabla f_i(x_i)$ and $\nabla \phi_i(x_i)$ are positively collinear.\\ 

\noindent\textbf{Proof strategy via orthogonal complements.} Set
$a:=\nabla f_i(x_i), b:=\nabla\phi_i(x_i).$ 
By assumption, $a\neq0$ and $b\neq0$.
To show that $a$ and $b$ are collinear, it suffices to show that $\operatorname{span}\{a\}
=\operatorname{span}\{b\}.$ In the following, we equivalently show by taking orthogonal complements that 
$a^\perp := \{d:\langle a,d\rangle=0\}$ and $b^\perp := \{d:\langle b,d\rangle=0\}$ coincide, i.e., 
$a^\perp=b^\perp.$ Finally, since $a$ and $b$ are both nonzero,
$a^\perp$ and $b^\perp$ are both $(d_i-1)$-dimensional subspaces, it suffices to show that $a^\perp \subseteq b^\perp$ 
to conclude the equality $a^\perp = b^\perp\,.$\\

\noindent\textbf{Proof of the inclusion $a^\perp \subseteq b^\perp$.} 
Fix any direction $d\in\mathbb R^{d_i}$ satisfying
$\langle a,d\rangle=0$. We prove that $\langle b, d\rangle = 0$ as well. 
For $\varepsilon>0$, define
$d_\varepsilon^+
:= d+\varepsilon a,
d_\varepsilon^-
:=
d-\varepsilon a.$ 
Since $x_i\in\operatorname{int}(\mathcal X_i)$, for every fixed
$\varepsilon>0$ and all sufficiently small $h>0$,
$x_i+hd_\varepsilon^\pm\in\mathcal X_i$.

By differentiability of $f_i$ (which follows from differentiability of $J_i)$,
\begin{align*}
f_i(x_i+hd_\varepsilon^+)-f_i(x_i)
=
h\langle a,d_\varepsilon^+\rangle+o(h)
=
h\varepsilon\|a\|^2+o(h)>0
\end{align*}
for all sufficiently small $h>0$.
By the ordinal-potential property,
\[
\phi_i(x_i+hd_\varepsilon^+)-\phi_i(x_i)>0
\]
for the same sufficiently small $h$.
Dividing by $h$ and letting $h\downarrow0$ gives
$\langle b,d+\varepsilon a\rangle\ge0.$ 
Similarly,
\begin{align*}
f_i(x_i+hd_\varepsilon^-)-f_i(x_i)
=
h\langle a,d_\varepsilon^-\rangle+o(h)
= -h\varepsilon\|a\|^2+o(h)<0
\end{align*}
for sufficiently small $h>0$.
Ordinal equivalence therefore gives
\[
\phi_i(x_i+hd_\varepsilon^-)-\phi_i(x_i)<0,
\]
and hence
$\langle b,d-\varepsilon a\rangle\le0.$
Letting $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ in the preceding two inequalities
yields
$\langle b,d\rangle\ge0$ and
$\langle b,d\rangle\le0,$ 
so $\langle b,d\rangle=0.$ 
This proves that $a^\perp \subseteq b^\perp$.\\ 

\noindent\textbf{Proof of positive collinearity.} 
We have proved that $a$ and $b$ are collinear: there exists
$\mu_i(x)\neq0$ such that $b=\mu_i(x)a.$
It remains to show that $\mu_i(x)>0$.
Consider the direction $a$. Since
$\langle a,a\rangle=\|a\|^2>0,$ 
differentiability gives
\[
f_i(x_i+ha)-f_i(x_i)>0
\]
for every sufficiently small $h>0$.
The ordinal-potential property therefore implies
\[
\phi_i(x_i+ha)-\phi_i(x_i)>0.
\]
Dividing by $h$ and letting $h\downarrow0$ yields
$\langle b,a\rangle\ge0.$ 
Since $b=\mu_i(x)a$,
$\langle b,a\rangle
=\mu_i(x)\|a\|^2.$ 
Thus $\mu_i(x)\ge0$. Because $b\neq0$, we have
$\mu_i(x)\neq0$, and consequently
$\mu_i(x)>0.$ 
Equivalently, there exists $\lambda_i(x)>0$ such that
\[
\nabla f_i(x_i)
=
\lambda_i(x)\nabla\phi_i(x_i).
\]

\noindent\textbf{Proof for the case $x_i\in\partial\mathcal X_i$.} 
It remains to treat $x_i\in\partial\mathcal X_i$. We will use the result for the case $x_i\in\operatorname{int}(\mathcal X_i)$ proved above. 
Fix $c_i\in\operatorname{int}(\mathcal X_i)$ which always exists since $\mathcal{X}_i$ has a nonempty interior by assumption, and let
$\varepsilon_n\downarrow0$ with $\varepsilon_n\in(0,1)$. Define
$x_i^n
:= (1-\varepsilon_n)x_i+\varepsilon_n c_i.$ 
By convexity and since $c_i\in\operatorname{int}(\mathcal X_i)$, 
we have $x_i^n\in\operatorname{int}(\mathcal X_i)$ 
for every $n$, and $x_i^n\to x_i$.

Set $x^n:=(x_i^n,x_{-i}).$ 
Since $\nabla_iJ_i(x)\neq0, \nabla_i\Phi(x)\neq0,$ 
continuity implies that both gradients remain nonzero at $x^n$
for all sufficiently large $n$. Applying the interior argument gives
\[
\frac{\nabla_iJ_i(x^n)}
{\|\nabla_iJ_i(x^n)\|}
=
\frac{\nabla_i\Phi(x^n)}
{\|\nabla_i\Phi(x^n)\|}.
\]
Passing to the limit and using continuity yields
\[
\frac{\nabla_iJ_i(x)}
{\|\nabla_iJ_i(x)\|}
=
\frac{\nabla_i\Phi(x)}
{\|\nabla_i\Phi(x)\|}.
\]
Since both gradients are nonzero,
\[
\nabla_iJ_i(x)
=
\frac{\|\nabla_iJ_i(x)\|}
{\|\nabla_i\Phi(x)\|}
\,\nabla_i\Phi(x).
\]
Thus (\ref{eq:positive-gradient-collinearity}) holds with
$\lambda_i(x)
:=
\frac{\|\nabla_iJ_i(x)\|}
{\|\nabla_i\Phi(x)\|}
>0.$ 
This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
\noindent\textbf{Proof of \ref{pot-dissipation}.} Let $x(t)$ be an absolutely
continuous solution of (\ref{eq:BNPGD}). By the differentiability of $\Phi$ and using the chain rule, we have for any $t \geq 0$,  
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:lyap1}
\frac{d}{dt}\Phi(x(t)) = \langle \dot{x}(t), \nabla \Phi(x(t)) \rangle = \sum_{i=1}^N \langle \dot{x}_i(t), \nabla_i \Phi(x(t)) \rangle = \sum_{i=1}^N \|\nabla_i \Phi(x(t))\| \cdot \langle \dot{x}_i(t), D_i(x(t)) \rangle\,, 
\end{equation}
where the last equality follows from using $D_i(x(t)) = \frac{\nabla_i \Phi(x(t))}{\|\nabla_i \Phi(x(t))\|}$ as proved in Lemma~\ref{lem:ordinal-potential-gradient-alignment}. 
When $\nabla_i\Phi(x)=0$, Assumption~\ref{as:non-degeneracy-ord-pot} gives $\nabla_iJ_i(x)=0$, hence $D_i(x)=0$ and that block contributes zero to the chain-rule identity. 

Now by Moreau's decomposition theorem applied, we can write for every $i \in [N]$ and any $t \geq 0, -D_i(x(t)) = \dot{x}_i(t) + n_i(t)$ where $\dot{x}_i(t) \in T_{\mathcal{X}_i}(x_i(t))$ and $n_i(t) \in N_{\mathcal{X}_i}(x_i(t))$. In particular, we have $\langle \dot{x}_i(t), n_i(t) \rangle = 0.$ (\ref{eq:BNPGD}) gives $\dot x_i=\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}\!\left(-D_i(x)\right)$ and using this in (\ref{eq:lyap1}) gives: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:lyap2}
\frac{d}{dt}\Phi(x(t)) = \sum_{i=1}^N \|\nabla_i \Phi(x(t))\| \cdot \langle \dot{x}_i(t), - \dot{x}_i(t) - n_i(t)  \rangle = -  \sum_{i=1}^N \|\nabla_i \Phi(x(t))\| \cdot \|\dot{x}_i(t)\|^2\,, 
\end{equation}
which concludes the proof of the first item using (\ref{eq:BNPGD}), i.e., $\dot{x}_i(t) = \Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}\!\left(-D_i(x)\right)$.\\

\noindent\textbf{Proof of \ref{pot-dissipation-interior}.} If the trajectory $x(t)$ remains in $\operatorname{int}(\mathcal X)$, it follows that $\dot{x}_i(t) = \Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}\!\left(-D_i(x)\right) = - D_i(x(t))$ as the projection is not active. Since $\|D_i(x(t))\| = 1$ when $\nabla_i \Phi(x(t)) \neq 0$, we obtain that for any $t$ s.t. $\nabla_i \Phi(x(t)) \neq 0$, 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:lyap2}
\frac{d}{dt}\Phi(x(t)) = -  \sum_{i=1}^N \|\nabla_i \Phi(x(t))\| = - \|\nabla \Phi(x(t))\|_{2,1}\,.  
\end{equation}

\noindent\textbf{Proof of \ref{fos}.} Let $T > 0$. Integrating the previous identity between $0$ and $T$, and rearranging the resulting identity yields: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:integ-pot}
\int_0^T \|\nabla \Phi(x(t))\|_{2,1} dt = \Phi(x(0)) - \Phi(x(T)) \leq \Phi(x(0)) - \Phi_{\inf}\,.
\end{equation}
As a consequence, 
\begin{equation}
\min_{0\le t\le T} \|\nabla\Phi(x(t))\|_{2,1} \leq \frac{1}{T} \int_0^T \|\nabla \Phi(x(t))\|_{2,1} dt \leq \frac{\Phi(x(0)) - \Phi_{\inf}}{T}\,.
\end{equation}

\noindent\textbf{Proof of \ref{convergence}.} 
A point $x^{\star}$ is a rest point of (\ref{eq:BNPGD}) if and only if: 
\begin{equation}
\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i^{\star})}\!\left(-D_i(x^{\star})\right) = 0\,, \quad \forall i \in [N]\,.
\end{equation}
Equivalently, $- D_i(x^\star) \in N_{\mathcal{X}_i}(x_i^\star)$, i.e., 
\begin{equation}
\langle D_i(x^{\star}), y_i - x_i^{\star} \rangle \geq 0\,, \quad \forall y_i \in \mathcal{X}_i, \forall i \in [N]\,.
\end{equation}
By (\ref{eq:ordinal-nash-gap}), this is equivalent to $\operatorname{Gap}_D(x^{\star}) = 0$, which by Proposition~\ref{prop:ordinal-gap-ne} is equivalent to $x^{\star} \in \operatorname{NE}(\mathcal{G})$ under Assumption~\ref{ass:block-pseudoconvex}.

Now it follows from (\ref{eq:integ-pot}) by taking $T \to +\infty$ that:  
\begin{equation}
\int_0^{+\infty} \|\nabla \Phi(x(t))\|_{2,1} dt < +\infty.
\end{equation}

We now prove that if $x(t)$ remains in a compact set $K\subset\operatorname{int}(\mathcal X)$ and $\nabla\Phi$ is Lipschitz on $K$, then $\|\nabla\Phi(x(t))\|_{2,1}\longrightarrow0.$ The proof of this statement follows from proving the following two claims: 
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*), leftmargin=*,itemsep=0pt,topsep=0pt]
\item For all $i \in [N], \int_0^\infty \|\nabla_i\Phi(x(t))\|\,dt<\infty\,.$
\item For any $i \in [N], g_i: t \mapsto \|\nabla_i\Phi(x(t))\|$ is uniformly continuous. 
\end{enumerate}
We start by proving each one of the statements before concluding. 

Along the trajectory, we have, for almost every $t\geq 0$,
\begin{equation}
\frac{d}{dt}\Phi(x(t))
=
\sum_{i=1}^N
\left\langle \nabla_i\Phi(x(t)),\dot{x}_i(t)\right\rangle
=
-\sum_{i=1}^N \|\nabla_i\Phi(x(t))\|.
\end{equation}
Hence, for every $T>0$,
\begin{equation}
\int_0^T
\sum_{i=1}^N\|\nabla_i\Phi(x(t))\|\,dt
=
\Phi(x(0))-\Phi(x(T)).
\end{equation}
Since $\Phi$ is bounded from below, it follows that for every $i \in [N],$
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:integrable-gi}
\int_0^\infty \|\nabla_i\Phi(x(t))\|\,dt<\infty\,, 
\end{equation}
which proves the first claim above. 

As for the second claim, observe first that $\|\dot{x}_i(t)\|\leq 1$ by definition of the normalized flow. 
It follows then that $\|\dot{x}(t)\|\leq \sqrt{N}$ for the Euclidean norm and as a consequence, for all $s,t\geq 0$, we have $\|x(t)-x(s)\| \leq \sqrt{N}\,|t-s|.$ 
Since $\nabla\Phi$ is $L$-Lipschitz on the compact set $K$ in which the trajectory lies, we obtain for all $s,t\geq 0$: 
\[
\begin{aligned}
|g_i(t)-g_i(s)| \leq \|\nabla_i\Phi(x(t))-\nabla_i\Phi(x(s))\|
\leq
\|\nabla\Phi(x(t))-\nabla\Phi(x(s))\| 
\leq
L\|x(t)-x(s)\|\\
\leq
L\sqrt{N}\,|t-s|\,, 
\end{aligned}
\]
which proves that $g_i$ is Lipschitz, and in particular uniformly continuous. This concludes the proof of the second claim above. 

We now show that $g_i(t)\to 0$ using a standard argument. We provide a detailed proof for completeness. Suppose otherwise for contradiction. Then there exist
$\varepsilon>0$ and a sequence $t_n\to\infty$ such that
$g_i(t_n)\geq \varepsilon$ for all $n$. 
By uniform continuity, there exists $\delta>0$, independent of $n$, such that
\[
|t-t_n|\leq\delta
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
g_i(t)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.
\]
Passing to a subsequence if necessary, we may assume that the intervals
$[t_n-\delta,t_n+\delta]$ are pairwise disjoint. It follows that
\[
\begin{aligned}
\int_0^\infty g_i(t)\,dt
\geq
\sum_n
\int_{t_n-\delta}^{t_n+\delta}g_i(t)\,dt 
\geq
\sum_n \varepsilon\delta
=
\infty,
\end{aligned}
\]
contradicting the integrability result proved above (\ref{eq:integrable-gi}). Therefore
$\lim_{t\to\infty}\|\nabla_i\Phi(x(t))\|=0.$

We conclude by proving that $\operatorname{dist}\bigl(x(t),\operatorname{NE}\bigr) \longrightarrow0.$
Suppose, by contradiction, that this limit statement does not hold. Then there
exist $\varepsilon>0$ and a sequence $t_k\to\infty$ such that
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{dist}(x(t_k),\operatorname{NE})\geq \varepsilon\,, 
\qquad \forall k \geq 0.
\end{equation}
Since $K$ is compact, after passing to a subsequence we may assume that $x(t_k)\to \bar x$ for some $\bar x\in K.$

By continuity of $\nabla_i\Phi$ and using the limit statement we already established, we obtain that for every player $i \in [N]$,
\[
\nabla_i\Phi(\bar x)
=
\lim_{k\to\infty}\nabla_i\Phi(x(t_k))
=
0.
\] 
Using then Assumption~\ref{as:non-degeneracy-ord-pot} gives $\nabla_i J_i(\bar{x}) = 0$ for every $i \in [N]$. 
Hence $D(\bar{x}) = 0$, which then implies that 
$\operatorname{Gap}_D(\bar{x}) = 0$ using (\ref{eq:ordinal-nash-gap}). We conclude then from Proposition~\ref{prop:ordinal-gap-ne} which uses Assumption~\ref{ass:block-pseudoconvex} that $\bar{x} \in \operatorname{NE}(\mathcal{G})$. 

Then $\operatorname{dist}(x(t_k),\operatorname{NE}) \leq \|x(t_k)-\bar x\| \longrightarrow 0$ 
because $\bar x$ is a Nash equilibrium and $x(t_k)\to \bar x$. This
contradicts $\operatorname{dist}(x(t_k),\operatorname{NE})\geq\varepsilon$.
Therefore $\operatorname{dist}(x(t),\operatorname{NE})\longrightarrow 0,$ as claimed.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Nonexpansiveness of projection gives, for every $y_i\in\mathcal X_i$,
\[
\|x_{i,t+1}-y_i\|^2
\leq
\|x_{i,t}-y_i\|^2
-2\eta\langle D_i(x_t),x_{i,t}-y_i\rangle
+\eta^2\|D_i(x_t)\|^2.
\]
Since $\|D_i(x_t)\|\leq1$, summing over $t$ and telescoping gives the result.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
On unbounded action sets (e.g., $\mathcal X_i=\mathbb R^{d_i}$), the uniform comparator supremum in $\operatorname{Reg}^D_{i,T}$ is generally infinite. Indeed,
it is infinite whenever $\sum_t D_i(x_t)\neq0$. This differs from standard external regret, where nonlinear utilities may make the cumulative comparator objective coercive and hence yield a finite infimum. Such quantitative growth away from an optimum is not encoded by the normalized preference field. On unbounded domains, we may use a comparator-dependent regret (i.e. for a fixed comparator $y_i$ rather than supremum in $\operatorname{Reg}^D_{i,T}$), or restrict the comparator class to a bounded neighborhood.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:finite-vs-infinitesimal-regret}
There exist a compact convex action set $\mathcal X\subset\mathbb R$, a
$C^\infty$ strongly convex cost function $J:\mathcal X\to\mathbb R$, a
sequence of comparison radii $\delta_t$ converging to zero, and a sequence of actions
$(x_t)_{t\geq1}$ such that all symmetric comparison queries
$x_t\pm\delta_t$ are feasible and, for every $K\geq1$,
\[
\operatorname{Reg}^{\delta}_{2K}
\leq \frac{\pi^2}{512},
\qquad\text{while}\qquad
\operatorname{Reg}^{D}_{2K}\geq 2K.
\]
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- head-3: body_md -->
We provide an overview of the proof to highlight the intuition. 
The construction exploits the fact that $D^\delta(x)\to D(x)$ only
pointwise away from stationary points. Consider
$J(x)=x^2-\frac14x^3$ on $\mathcal X=[-1,1]$. For every small $x>0$,
$J'(x)>0$, so the infinitesimal direction is $D(x)=1$. On the other hand,
the symmetric finite difference is
\[
J(x+\delta)-J(x-\delta)
=
2\delta
\left(
2x-\frac34x^2-\frac14\delta^2
\right).
\]
Thus the first-order contribution is of size $\delta x$, while the
finite-resolution correction is of size $\delta^3$. Choosing
$x=\Theta(\delta^2)$ makes these terms comparable, so the sign of the finite
comparison can differ from the infinitesimal gradient direction even as
$\delta\downarrow0$.

For each resolution $\varepsilon_k$, we choose two positive actions on opposite
sides of this sign-change threshold:
\[
x_{2k-1}=\frac{\varepsilon_k^2}{16},
\qquad
x_{2k}=\frac{\varepsilon_k^2}{4},
\qquad
\delta_{2k-1}=\delta_{2k}=\varepsilon_k.
\]
Then $D(x_{2k-1})=D(x_{2k})=1$, whereas the corresponding
finite-resolution directions satisfy
\[
D^{\delta_{2k-1}}(x_{2k-1})=-1,
\qquad
D^{\delta_{2k}}(x_{2k})=1.
\]
Hence the finite-resolution directions cancel pairwise, so the comparator term
in $\operatorname{Reg}^\delta$ cancels as well; the remaining contribution is of order
$\sum_k \varepsilon_k^2$. Taking $\varepsilon_k\asymp 1/k$ makes this sum
finite. By contrast, the infinitesimal directions are equal to $+1$ at every
round, so the fixed comparator $y=-1$ yields a unit-order advantage per round
and therefore linear $\operatorname{Reg}^D$. The example shows that vanishing comparison
radii alone do not ensure that the finite-resolution field tracks the
infinitesimal one along trajectories approaching the stationary set.

\begin{proof}
Consider the one-dimensional action set
$\mathcal X=[-1,1]$ 
and the cost function $J: \mathcal{X} \to \mathbb{R}$ defined for any $x \in \mathcal{X}$ by: 
\begin{equation}
J(x)=x^2-\frac14x^3.
\end{equation}
Since the first and second derivatives of $J$ are respectively given by 
$J'(x)=2x-\frac34x^2,
J''(x)=2-\frac32x,$
we have $J''(x)\geq 1/2$ for every $x\in[-1,1]$. Hence $J$ is
$1/2$-strongly convex on $\mathcal X$. Moreover, for every $x\in(0,1]$,
$J'(x) = x\left(2-\frac34x\right)>0,$ 
so the infinitesimal preference direction satisfies
$D(x)=1$ for any $x \in (0,1]\,.$ 

In dimension one, $\mathbb S^0=\{-1,+1\}$ and $\kappa_1=1$. Therefore,
for every feasible $x\pm\delta$,
\[
D^\delta(x)
=
\operatorname{sgn}\!\left(J(x+\delta)-J(x-\delta)\right).
\]
Indeed, if
$s=\operatorname{sgn}(J(x+\delta)-J(x-\delta))$, then the two possible
values of $S^\delta(x,U)U$, corresponding to $U=1$ and $U=-1$, are both
equal to $s$.

A direct calculation yields
\begin{equation}
J(x+\delta)-J(x-\delta)
=
4x\delta
-\frac14\left(6x^2\delta+2\delta^3\right) 
=
2\delta
\left(
2x-\frac34x^2-\frac14\delta^2
\right).
\label{eq:finite-inf-separation-central-diff}
\end{equation}

For every $k\geq1$, let
$
\varepsilon_k:=\frac{1}{4k},
\delta_{2k-1}=\delta_{2k}:=\varepsilon_k,$ 
and define
\[
x_{2k-1}:=\frac{\varepsilon_k^2}{16},
\qquad
x_{2k}:=\frac{\varepsilon_k^2}{4}.
\]
Clearly, $\delta_t \to 0$, and all query points
$x_t\pm\delta_t$ belong to $\mathcal X$. Since $x_t>0$ for every $t$,
\[
D(x_t)=1.
\]

For the first action in each pair, using
(\ref{eq:finite-inf-separation-central-diff}),
\begin{equation}
2x_{2k-1}
-\frac34x_{2k-1}^2
-\frac14\varepsilon_k^2
=
\frac{\varepsilon_k^2}{8}
-\frac{3\varepsilon_k^4}{1024}
-\frac{\varepsilon_k^2}{4}\\
=
-\frac{\varepsilon_k^2}{8}
-\frac{3\varepsilon_k^4}{1024}
<0,
\end{equation}
and hence
\[
D^{\delta_{2k-1}}(x_{2k-1})=-1.
\]
For the second action,
\begin{equation}
2x_{2k}
-\frac34x_{2k}^2
-\frac14\varepsilon_k^2
=
\frac{\varepsilon_k^2}{2}
-\frac{3\varepsilon_k^4}{64}
-\frac{\varepsilon_k^2}{4}
=
\frac{\varepsilon_k^2}{4}
-\frac{3\varepsilon_k^4}{64}
>0,
\end{equation}
where the last inequality follows from $\varepsilon_k\leq1/4$. Thus
\[
D^{\delta_{2k}}(x_{2k})=1.
\]
The finite-resolution directions therefore cancel pairwise:
\[
D^{\delta_{2k-1}}(x_{2k-1})
+
D^{\delta_{2k}}(x_{2k})
=0.
\]

For $T=2K$, the finite-resolution regret is consequently
\begin{align*}
\operatorname{Reg}^\delta_{2K}
&=
\sup_{y\in[-1,1]}
\sum_{t=1}^{2K}
D^{\delta_t}(x_t)(x_t-y)\\
&=
\sum_{k=1}^K
\left(
-x_{2k-1}+x_{2k}
\right)\\
&=
\frac{3}{16}
\sum_{k=1}^K\varepsilon_k^2\\
&=
\frac{3}{256}
\sum_{k=1}^K\frac{1}{k^2}\\
&\leq
\frac{3}{256}\frac{\pi^2}{6}
=
\frac{\pi^2}{512}.
\end{align*}

On the other hand, since $D(x_t)=1$ for every $t$,
\begin{align*}
\operatorname{Reg}^D_{2K}
=
\sup_{y\in[-1,1]}
\sum_{t=1}^{2K}(x_t-y)
=
\sum_{t=1}^{2K}x_t+2K,
\end{align*}
where the supremum is attained at $y=-1$. Therefore
\[
\operatorname{Reg}^D_{2K}\geq2K.
\]
This proves the claim.
\end{proof}
<!-- end head-3 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Consider the one-dimensional action set
$\mathcal X=[-1,1]$ 
and the cost function $J: \mathcal{X} \to \mathbb{R}$ defined for any $x \in \mathcal{X}$ by: 
\begin{equation}
J(x)=x^2-\frac14x^3.
\end{equation}
Since the first and second derivatives of $J$ are respectively given by 
$J'(x)=2x-\frac34x^2,
J''(x)=2-\frac32x,$
we have $J''(x)\geq 1/2$ for every $x\in[-1,1]$. Hence $J$ is
$1/2$-strongly convex on $\mathcal X$. Moreover, for every $x\in(0,1]$,
$J'(x) = x\left(2-\frac34x\right)>0,$ 
so the infinitesimal preference direction satisfies
$D(x)=1$ for any $x \in (0,1]\,.$ 

In dimension one, $\mathbb S^0=\{-1,+1\}$ and $\kappa_1=1$. Therefore,
for every feasible $x\pm\delta$,
\[
D^\delta(x)
=
\operatorname{sgn}\!\left(J(x+\delta)-J(x-\delta)\right).
\]
Indeed, if
$s=\operatorname{sgn}(J(x+\delta)-J(x-\delta))$, then the two possible
values of $S^\delta(x,U)U$, corresponding to $U=1$ and $U=-1$, are both
equal to $s$.

A direct calculation yields
\begin{equation}
J(x+\delta)-J(x-\delta)
=
4x\delta
-\frac14\left(6x^2\delta+2\delta^3\right) 
=
2\delta
\left(
2x-\frac34x^2-\frac14\delta^2
\right).
\label{eq:finite-inf-separation-central-diff}
\end{equation}

For every $k\geq1$, let
$
\varepsilon_k:=\frac{1}{4k},
\delta_{2k-1}=\delta_{2k}:=\varepsilon_k,$ 
and define
\[
x_{2k-1}:=\frac{\varepsilon_k^2}{16},
\qquad
x_{2k}:=\frac{\varepsilon_k^2}{4}.
\]
Clearly, $\delta_t \to 0$, and all query points
$x_t\pm\delta_t$ belong to $\mathcal X$. Since $x_t>0$ for every $t$,
\[
D(x_t)=1.
\]

For the first action in each pair, using
(\ref{eq:finite-inf-separation-central-diff}),
\begin{equation}
2x_{2k-1}
-\frac34x_{2k-1}^2
-\frac14\varepsilon_k^2
=
\frac{\varepsilon_k^2}{8}
-\frac{3\varepsilon_k^4}{1024}
-\frac{\varepsilon_k^2}{4}\\
=
-\frac{\varepsilon_k^2}{8}
-\frac{3\varepsilon_k^4}{1024}
<0,
\end{equation}
and hence
\[
D^{\delta_{2k-1}}(x_{2k-1})=-1.
\]
For the second action,
\begin{equation}
2x_{2k}
-\frac34x_{2k}^2
-\frac14\varepsilon_k^2
=
\frac{\varepsilon_k^2}{2}
-\frac{3\varepsilon_k^4}{64}
-\frac{\varepsilon_k^2}{4}
=
\frac{\varepsilon_k^2}{4}
-\frac{3\varepsilon_k^4}{64}
>0,
\end{equation}
where the last inequality follows from $\varepsilon_k\leq1/4$. Thus
\[
D^{\delta_{2k}}(x_{2k})=1.
\]
The finite-resolution directions therefore cancel pairwise:
\[
D^{\delta_{2k-1}}(x_{2k-1})
+
D^{\delta_{2k}}(x_{2k})
=0.
\]

For $T=2K$, the finite-resolution regret is consequently
\begin{align*}
\operatorname{Reg}^\delta_{2K}
&=
\sup_{y\in[-1,1]}
\sum_{t=1}^{2K}
D^{\delta_t}(x_t)(x_t-y)\\
&=
\sum_{k=1}^K
\left(
-x_{2k-1}+x_{2k}
\right)\\
&=
\frac{3}{16}
\sum_{k=1}^K\varepsilon_k^2\\
&=
\frac{3}{256}
\sum_{k=1}^K\frac{1}{k^2}\\
&\leq
\frac{3}{256}\frac{\pi^2}{6}
=
\frac{\pi^2}{512}.
\end{align*}

On the other hand, since $D(x_t)=1$ for every $t$,
\begin{align*}
\operatorname{Reg}^D_{2K}
=
\sup_{y\in[-1,1]}
\sum_{t=1}^{2K}(x_t-y)
=
\sum_{t=1}^{2K}x_t+2K,
\end{align*}
where the supremum is attained at $y=-1$. Therefore
\[
\operatorname{Reg}^D_{2K}\geq2K.
\]
This proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
\noindent\textbf{Proof of (i).} Observe that strictly increasing transformations preserve the sign, i.e., for any strictly increasing function $\phi_i: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$, any $x \in \mathcal{X}, U_i$ s.t. $x_i \pm \delta U_i \in \mathcal{X}_i$,  
\begin{equation*}
    \operatorname{sgn}(J_i(x_i + \delta U_i, x_{-i}) - J_i(x_i - \delta U_i, x_{-i})) = 
    \operatorname{sgn}(\phi_i(J_i(x_i + \delta U_i, x_{-i})) - \phi_i(J_i(x_i - \delta U_i, x_{-i})))\,.
\end{equation*}
This implies that $S_i^{\delta}(x,U_i)$ is invariant under transformations of the cost functions $J_i$ by strictly increasing functions. As a consequence,  the \emph{$\delta$-comparison field} $D_i^\delta(x)
:=\frac{1}{\kappa_{d_i}}\mathbb E_{U_i}\left[S_i^\delta(x,U_i)U_i\right]$ defined in (\ref{eq:delta-comparison-dir}) is also invariant.\\

\noindent\textbf{Proof of (ii).} 
The desired bound follows from: 
\begin{equation}
\|D_i^\delta(x)\|
=
\sup_{\|v\|=1}
\frac{1}{\kappa_{d_i}}
\mathbb E\!\left[
S_i^\delta(x,U_i)\langle U_i,v\rangle
\right]
\leq
\frac{1}{\kappa_{d_i}}
\sup_{\|v\|=1}
\mathbb E\!\left[
|\langle U_i,v\rangle|
\right]
=1\,,
\end{equation}
where the first equality uses the fact that $\|z\| = \sup_{\|v\| =1} \langle z,v \rangle$ (which follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality) and the definition of $D_i^{\delta}(x)$ in (\ref{eq:delta-comparison-dir}), and the last inequality uses Lemma 3.1 in~\citet{fu-wang-yan23minimax-spherical} (which relies on rotational invariance).\\ 

\noindent\textbf{Proof of (iii).} Suppose $\nabla_i J_i(x) \neq 0.$ Then differentiability of $J_i(\cdot, x_{-i})$ gives, for every $u$,
\[
\frac{
J_i(x_i+\delta u,x_{-i})
-
J_i(x_i-\delta u,x_{-i})
}{2\delta}
\underset{\delta \to 0}{\longrightarrow}
\langle \nabla_iJ_i(x),u\rangle.
\]
For all $u$ outside the measure-zero great sphere $\{u:\langle \nabla_i J_i(x),u\rangle=0\}$,
\[
S_i^\delta(x,u)
\underset{\delta \to 0}{\longrightarrow}
\operatorname{sgn}(\langle \nabla_iJ_i(x),u\rangle).
\]
Dominated convergence therefore yields
\[
D_i^\delta(x) = \frac{1}{\kappa_{d_i}}
\mathbb E_{U_i}
\left[
S_i^\delta(x,U_i)U_i
\right]
\underset{\delta \to 0}{\longrightarrow}
\frac{1}{\kappa_{d_i}}
\mathbb E
\left[
\operatorname{sgn}(\langle \nabla_iJ_i(x),U_i\rangle) U_i
\right].
\]
By rotational symmetry, the expectation on the right must be collinear with $\nabla_i J_i(x)$. Taking its inner product with $\nabla_i J_i(x)/\|\nabla_i J_i(x)\|$ gives
$\mathbb E
\left|
\left\langle
U_i,\frac{\nabla_i J_i(x)}{\|\nabla_i J_i(x)\|}
\right\rangle
\right| =
\kappa_{d_i},$ and hence 
\begin{equation*}
\frac{1}{\kappa_{d_i}}
\mathbb E\left[\operatorname{sgn}(\langle \nabla_iJ_i(x),U_i\rangle) U_i\right] 
= \frac{\nabla_i J_i(x)}{\|\nabla_i J_i(x)\|} = D_i(x)\,.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Conditional on $U_{i,t}$,
\begin{align}
\mathbb E[Y_{i,t}\mid U_{i,t},\mathcal F_t]
&=\mathbb E[\xi_{i,t} S_i^{\delta_t}(x_t,U_{i,t})\mid U_{i,t},\mathcal F_t] \nonumber\\
&= \left(\frac 12 + \nu_i\right) S_i^{\delta_t}(x_t,U_{i,t}) - \left(1 -\frac 12 - \nu_i \right)S_i^{\delta_t}(x_t,U_{i,t})\nonumber\\
&= 2\nu_i S_i^{\delta_t}(x_t,U_{i,t}).\nonumber
\end{align}
Hence
\[
\mathbb E[
\widehat D_{i,t}
\mid\mathcal F_t]
= \mathbb E\left[ \frac{Y_{i,t}}{2 \nu_i \kappa_{d_i}} U_{i,t}
\mid\mathcal F_t \right]
=
\frac{1}{\kappa_{d_i}}
\mathbb E_{U_{i,t}}
[
S_i^{\delta_t}(x_t,U_{i,t})U_{i,t}]
=
D_i^{\delta_t}(x_t).
\]
Moreover, $\|U_{i,t}\|=1$ and $|Y_{i,t}|\leq1$, so
\[
\|\widehat D_{i,t}\|^2
= \left\|\frac{Y_{i,t}}{2 \nu_i \kappa_{d_i}} U_{i,t} \right\|^2
\leq
\frac{1}{4\nu_i^2\kappa_{d_i}^2} = G_i^2\,.
\]
Taking conditional expectation concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
For exact comparisons,
$G_i =\kappa_{d_i}^{-1} =
\sqrt{\frac{\pi d_i}{2}}\,(1+o(1)).$
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Let $y_i\in\mathcal X_i$. Projection nonexpansiveness gives
\begin{equation}
\|z_{i,t+1}-y_i\|^2
\leq
\|z_{i,t}-y_i\|^2
-2\eta_i
\langle\widehat D_{i,t},z_{i,t}-y_i\rangle
+
\eta_i^2\|\widehat D_{i,t}\|^2.
\end{equation}
Rearranging and summing,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:pathwise-bound}
\sum_{t=1}^T
\langle\widehat D_{i,t},z_{i,t}-y_i\rangle
\leq
\frac{\|z_{i,1}-y_i\|^2}{2\eta_i}
+
\frac{\eta_i}{2}
\sum_{t=1}^T
\|\widehat D_{i,t}\|^2
\leq
\frac{R_i^2}{2\eta_i}
+
\frac{\eta_i}{2}
\sum_{t=1}^T
\|\widehat D_{i,t}\|^2.
\end{equation}
Now, introducing the sequence $(x_{i,t})$, we obtain: 
\begin{equation}
\sum_{t=1}^T
\langle\widehat D_{i,t},x_{i,t}-y_i\rangle 
=  \sum_{t=1}^T
\langle\widehat D_{i,t},z_{i,t}-y_i\rangle 
+ \sum_{t=1}^T
\langle\widehat D_{i,t},x_{i,t}-z_{i,t}\rangle\,.
\end{equation}
Taking expectations, using $\mathbb E[
\widehat D_{i,t}\mid\mathcal F_t]
=
D_i^{\delta_t}(x_t)$, $\mathbb E[
\|\widehat D_{i,t}\|^2
\mid\mathcal F_t]
\leq
G_i^2$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:one-bit-estimator}), (\ref{eq:pathwise-bound}), 
and noting that $z_{i,t}-y_i$ and $x_{i,t}-z_{i,t}$ are $\mathcal{F}_t$-measurable yields: 
\[
\mathbb E \left[
\sum_{t=1}^T
\langle
D_i^{\delta_t}(x_t),
x_{i,t}-y_i
\rangle \right]
\leq
\frac{R_i^2}{2\eta_i}
+
\frac{\eta_iG_i^2T}{2} 
+ \mathbb E \left[\sum_{t=1}^T \langle D_i^{\delta_t}(x_t), x_{i,t} - z_{i,t} \rangle\right]\,.
\]
Since this is true for every fixed $y_i \in \mathcal{X}_i$, taking the supremum gives: 
\begin{equation}
\overline{\operatorname{Reg}}_{i,T}^{\delta}
\leq
\frac{R_i^2}{2\eta_i}
+
\frac{\eta_i G_i^2T}{2} 
+  \mathbb E \left[\sum_{t=1}^T \langle D_i^{\delta_t}(x_t), x_{i,t} - z_{i,t} \rangle \right]\,.
\end{equation}
To obtain the first desired inequality, it remains to use Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for the last inner product, with the fact that $\|D_i^{\delta}(x_t)\| \leq 1$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:comparison-field}) and $\|x_{i,t}-z_{i,t}\| \le \frac{R_i}{\rho_i}\delta_t$. We obtain: 
\begin{equation}
\overline{\operatorname{Reg}}_{i,T}^{\delta}
\leq
\frac{R_i^2}{2\eta_i}
+
\frac{\eta_i G_i^2T}{2} 
+ \frac{R_i}{\rho_i} \sum_{t=1}^T \delta_t\,.
\end{equation}
Setting $\eta_i=\frac{R_i}{G_i\sqrt T}$ and $\delta_t = \frac{\delta}{\sqrt{T}}$ (with $\delta \in (0, \rho)$) 
gives:
\begin{equation}
\overline{\operatorname{Reg}}_{i,T}^{\delta}
\leq
R_iG_i\sqrt T 
+ R_i \frac{\rho}{\rho_i} \sqrt{T}
\leq R_i(G_i +1) \sqrt{T}
= O\!\left(
\frac{R_i\sqrt{d_iT}}{\nu_i}
\right)\,.
\end{equation}

To control the realized regret, define for all $i \in [N]$, the martingale difference sequence $(M_{i,t})$:  
\[
M_{i,t}
:= 
D_i^{\delta_t}(x_t)-\widehat D_{i,t}\,, \quad \forall t \geq 0\,.
\]
For an arbitrary reference point $y_i^0\in\mathcal X_i$, every $y_i\in\mathcal X_i$ and any $T > 0$, 
\[
\sum_{t=1}^T
\langle
M_{i,t},
x_{i,t}-y_i
\rangle
=
\sum_{t=1}^T
\langle
M_{i,t},
x_{i,t}-y_i^0
\rangle
+
\left\langle
\sum_{t=1}^TM_{i,t},
y_i^0-y_i
\right\rangle.
\]
Hence, for any $i \in [N]$ and any $T > 0$,
\begin{align}
\operatorname{Reg}_{i,T}^{\delta} &=  \sup_{y_i \in \mathcal{X}_i}
\sum_{t=1}^T
\langle
D_{i}^{\delta_t}(x_t),
x_{i,t}-y_i
\rangle \nonumber\\
&= \sup_{y_i \in \mathcal{X}_i}
\sum_{t=1}^T
\langle
\widehat{D}_{i,t},
x_{i,t}-y_i
\rangle + \sum_{t=1}^T
\langle
M_{i,t},
x_{i,t}-y_i
\rangle \nonumber\\
&= \sup_{y_i \in \mathcal{X}_i}
\sum_{t=1}^T
\langle
\widehat{D}_{i,t},
x_{i,t}-y_i
\rangle 
+ \sum_{t=1}^T
\langle
M_{i,t},
x_{i,t}-y_i^0
\rangle + 
\sum_{t=1}^T
\langle
M_{i,t},
y_i^0-y_i
\rangle   \nonumber\\
&\leq \sup_{y_i \in \mathcal{X}_i}
\sum_{t=1}^T
\langle
\widehat D_{i,t},
x_{i,t}-y_i
\rangle
+
A_T
+
R_i \cdot\left\|
\sum_{t=1}^TM_{i,t}
\right\|,  \label{eq:master-realized-regret}
\end{align}
where $A_T
:=  \sum_{t=1}^T
\langle M_{i,t},x_{i,t}-y_i^0 \rangle.$

By the pathwise projection inequality proved above in (\ref{eq:pathwise-bound}), 
\[ \sup_{y_i\in\mathcal X_i} \sum_{t=1}^T \langle \widehat D_{i,t}, x_{i,t}-y_i \rangle \leq \frac{R_i^2}{2\eta_i} + \frac{\eta_i}{2} \sum_{t=1}^T \|\widehat D_{i,t}\|^2 
+ \sum_{t=1}^T \langle \widehat D_{i,t}, x_{i,t}-z_{i,t} \rangle
\] 
Moreover, by construction,
$\|x_{i,t}-z_{i,t}\|
\le
\frac{R_i}{\rho_i}\delta_t,$
and, from the definition of the single-comparison estimator,
$\|\widehat D_{i,t}\|
=
\frac{|Y_{i,t}|}{2\nu_i\kappa_{d_i}}
\le
G_i$ almost surely.
Hence, using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have: 
\begin{equation}
\sum_{t=1}^T
\langle
\widehat D_{i,t},
x_{i,t}-z_{i,t}
\rangle
\le 
\sum_{t=1}^T \|\widehat D_{i,t}\| 
\cdot \|x_{i,t}-z_{i,t}\|
\le
\frac{R_iG_i}{\rho_i}
\sum_{t=1}^T\delta_t.
\end{equation}
Moreover, since $x_{i,t}$ is $\mathcal F_t$-measurable and $\mathbb E[M_{i,t}\mid\mathcal F_t]=0$, $\mathbb E[A_T]=0.$

We also have, using
$M_{i,t} = D_i^{\delta_t}(x_t)-\widehat D_{i,t},
D_i^{\delta_t}(x_t) = \mathbb E[\widehat D_{i,t}\mid\mathcal F_t]$,
we obtain
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E[
\|M_{i,t}\|^2
\mid\mathcal F_t]
&=
\mathbb E\!\left[
\left\|
\widehat D_{i,t}
-
\mathbb E[\widehat D_{i,t}\mid\mathcal F_t]
\right\|^2
\Bigm|\mathcal F_t
\right] \\
&=
\mathbb E[
\|\widehat D_{i,t}\|^2
\mid\mathcal F_t]
-
\left\|
\mathbb E[
\widehat D_{i,t}\mid\mathcal F_t]
\right\|^2 \\
&\leq
\mathbb E[
\|\widehat D_{i,t}\|^2
\mid\mathcal F_t]
\leq
G_i^2\,, 
\end{aligned}
\]
where the last inequality follows from using Lemma~\ref{lem:one-bit-estimator}. 

By martingale orthogonality, 
\[ \mathbb E \left\| \sum_{t=1}^T M_{i,t} \right\|^2 = \sum_{t=1}^T \mathbb E\|M_{i,t}\|^2 \leq G_i^2T. \] 

Therefore, by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, 
\[ \mathbb E \left\| \sum_{t=1}^T M_{i,t} \right\| \leq G_i\sqrt T. \] 

Taking expectations in (\ref{eq:master-realized-regret}) therefore yields 
\[ \mathbb E[\operatorname{Reg}_{i,T}^{\delta}] \leq \frac{R_i^2}{2\eta_i} + \frac{\eta_iG_i^2T}{2} + R_iG_i\sqrt T +
\frac{R_iG_i}{\rho_i}
\sum_{t=1}^T\delta_t. \] 
With $\eta_i=\frac{R_i}{G_i\sqrt T},$ the first two terms sum to $R_iG_i\sqrt T$, and with $\delta_t = \frac{\delta}{\sqrt{T}}$ the last term is bounded by $R_i G_i \sqrt{T}$ as well. Hence, 
\[\mathbb E[\operatorname{Reg}_{i,T}^{\delta}] \leq 3R_iG_i\sqrt T = O\!\left( \frac{R_i\sqrt{d_iT}}{\nu_i} \right). 
\] 

Finally, we bound the positive part. Since the right-hand side of (\ref{eq:master-realized-regret}), apart from $A_T$, consists of nonnegative terms, 
\[ [\operatorname{Reg}_{i,T}^{\delta}]_+ \leq \frac{R_i^2}{2\eta_i} + \frac{\eta_i}{2} \sum_{t=1}^T \|\widehat D_{i,t}\|^2 
+\frac{R_iG_i}{\rho_i}\sum_{t=1}^T\delta_t
+|A_T| + R_i \left\| \sum_{t=1}^T M_{i,t} \right\|. \] 
It remains to control $A_T$. Note first that its increments form a scalar martingale difference sequence. 
To see this, define 
$Z_t :=\left\langle M_{i,t},x_{i,t}-y_i^0 \right\rangle,$ so that $A_T=\sum_{t=1}^T Z_t.$ 
Since $x_{i,t}$ is $\mathcal F_t$-measurable and
$\mathbb E[M_{i,t}\mid\mathcal F_t]=0,$ 
we have $\mathbb E[Z_t\mid\mathcal F_t] = \left\langle \mathbb E[M_{i,t}\mid\mathcal F_t],
x_{i,t}-y_i^0 \right\rangle =0.$
Thus $(Z_t)$ is a scalar martingale difference sequence.
Expanding the square gives
\[
A_T^2
=
\sum_{t=1}^T Z_t^2
+
2\sum_{1\leq s<t\leq T} Z_sZ_t.
\]
For $s<t$, $Z_s$ is $\mathcal F_t$-measurable, so by the tower property,
$\mathbb E[Z_sZ_t] = 
\mathbb E\!\left[
\mathbb E[Z_sZ_t\mid\mathcal F_t]
\right] = \mathbb E\!\left[
Z_s\,\mathbb E[Z_t\mid\mathcal F_t]
\right]
=0.$
Therefore the cross terms vanish, and
\[
\mathbb E[A_T^2]
=
\sum_{t=1}^T
\mathbb E[Z_t^2].
\]

By Cauchy--Schwarz inequality,
\[
Z_t^2
=
\left\langle
M_{i,t},
x_{i,t}-y_i^0
\right\rangle^2
\leq
\|M_{i,t}\|^2
\|x_{i,t}-y_i^0\|^2.
\]
Since $x_{i,t},y_i^0\in\mathcal X_i$,
$\|x_{i,t}-y_i^0\|
\leq
R_i,$
and hence
\[
\mathbb E[A_T^2]
\leq
R_i^2
\sum_{t=1}^T
\mathbb E\|M_{i,t}\|^2 \leq R_i^2G_i^2T.
\]
Therefore, by Cauchy--Schwarz inequality,
\[
\mathbb E[|A_T|]
\leq
\sqrt{\mathbb E[A_T^2]}
\leq
R_iG_i\sqrt T.
\]

Thus, it follows that: 
\[ \mathbb E\bigl[[\operatorname{Reg}_{i,T}^{\delta}]_+\bigr] \leq \frac{R_i^2}{2\eta_i} + \frac{\eta_iG_i^2T}{2} + 2R_iG_i\sqrt T 
+\frac{R_iG_i}{\rho_i}\sum_{t=1}^T\delta_t
. \] 
Choosing again $\eta_i=\frac{R_i}{G_i\sqrt T}$ and $\delta_t = \frac{\delta}{\sqrt{T}}$ with $\delta \in (0, \rho)$ gives 
\[\mathbb E\bigl[[\operatorname{Reg}_{i,T}^{\delta}]_+\bigr] \leq 4R_iG_i\sqrt T = O\!\left( \frac{R_i\sqrt{d_iT}}{\nu_i} \right).\] 
This proves the claimed bound.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
To compare the finite-resolution guarantee with the infinitesimal benchmark,
let $b_{i,t}:= \|D_i^{\delta_t}(x_t)-D_i(x_t)\|.$
Then $\operatorname{Reg}_{i,T}^{D} \leq \operatorname{Reg}_{i,T}^{\delta}
+R_i\sum_{t=1}^T b_{i,t}.$
Uniform control of $b_{i,t}$ is impossible in general near vanishing
gradients; the normalized infinitesimal field is nonsmooth in general and may change sharply even when the
underlying utilities are smooth. This is why $D^\delta$ is the natural
oracle-matched benchmark at finite comparison resolution.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:majewski-et-al4.2}
Let $\mathcal K\subset\mathbb R^d$ be nonempty, compact, and convex,
let $\mathcal U\supseteq\mathcal K$ be open, and let
$F:\mathcal U\rightrightarrows\mathbb R^d$ be a set-valued mapping. 
Consider a recursion of the form
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:generic-ppad}
z_{t+1}
=
\Pi_{\mathcal K}
\left[
z_t+\eta_t
\bigl(f_t+e_{t+1}+r_t\bigr)
\right],
\qquad
f_t\in F(y_t)\,,
\end{equation}
where the set-valued mapping $F$, the stepsize sequence $(\eta_t)$, the noise sequence $(e_t)$ and the bias sequence $(r_t)$ satisfy the following assumptions:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(A\arabic*)]
\item \label{as:set-val-mapping}
$F$ is upper hemicontinuous, locally bounded, and has nonempty
compact convex values. 
\item \label{as:stepsize}
$\eta_t>0$, $\eta_t\to0$, and
$\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\eta_t=\infty.$
\item \label{as:bias-and-stoch-error}
$r_t\to0$ and
$\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\eta_t e_{t+1}$ 
converges.
\item \label{as:state-y-to-zt}
$y_t\in\mathcal U$ and
$\|y_t-z_t\|\to0.$
\item \label{as:bounded-error}
$\sup_t\|e_{t+1}\|<\infty$.
\end{enumerate} 

Suppose that $V\in C^1(\mathcal U)$ and that there exists an
upper semicontinuous function $U:\mathcal U\to\mathbb R$ such that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:majewski-specialized-Lyapunov}
\left\langle
\nabla V(x),
\Pi_{T_{\mathcal K}(x)}u
\right\rangle
\le
U(x)
\le0,
\qquad
\forall x\in\mathcal K,\quad
\forall u\in F(x).
\end{equation}
Let $\mathcal S:=\{x\in\mathcal K:U(x)=0\}.$ 
Then the image under $V$ of the limit set of $(z_t)$ is a compact
interval contained in $V(\mathcal S)$. If
$\operatorname{int}_{\mathbb R}V(\mathcal S)=\varnothing,$
then
\[
\operatorname{dist}(z_t,\mathcal S)\longrightarrow0.
\]
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- head-4: body_md -->
To apply Theorem~\ref{thm:majewski-et-al4.2}, we proceed in four steps:
\begin{enumerate}
    
\item \textbf{Completion of the limiting normalized field.}
We complete the normalized potential field at zero-gradient blocks and
show that the resulting set-valued field is upper hemicontinuous,
locally bounded, and has nonempty compact convex values
(definition in~(\ref{def:completed-potential-field}) and
Lemma~\ref{lem:completed-field-regularity}).

\item \textbf{Graph consistency and stochastic-approximation representation.}
We show that the finite-resolution comparison fields approach the
completed limiting field in the sense of graphs
(Lemma~\ref{lem:graph-consistency-finite-resolution}). This allows us
to represent our (\ref{eq:cngp}) algorithm as a projected perturbed approximate discretization
of the projected differential inclusion
\[
\dot{x}(t)
\in
\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X}(x(t))}F(x(t))\,,
\]
see Lemma~\ref{lem:cngp-ppad}. 

\item \textbf{Lyapunov analysis and potential stationarity.}
We show that the ordinal potential $\Phi$ is a Lyapunov function for
the projected limiting inclusion and identify the zero set of its
dissipation with the constrained stationary points of the potential
(Lemma~\ref{lem:lyapunov-ineq}).

\item \textbf{From potential stationarity to Nash equilibrium.}
We establish first-order alignment between the players' costs and the
ordinal potential under Assumption~\ref{as:non-degeneracy-ord-pot} and, using blockwise pseudoconvexity, show that the
potential stationary set coincides with the Nash equilibrium set
(Lemmas~\ref{lem:ordinal-potential-gradient-alignment}
and~\ref{lem:potential-stationarity-ne}). Theorem~\ref{thm:majewski-et-al4.2}
then yields the desired almost-sure convergence result.
\end{enumerate}


Let $\mathcal U$ be the open neighborhood of $\mathcal X$ on which $\Phi$ is $C^1$. 
For every $i\in[N]$, let
$g_i(x):=\nabla_i\Phi(x),$
and define
$\mathcal D_i:\mathcal U\rightrightarrows\mathbb R^{d_i}$ by
\begin{equation}
\label{def:completed-potential-field}
\mathcal D_i(x)
:=
\begin{cases}
\left\{
\dfrac{g_i(x)}{\|g_i(x)\|}
\right\},
&
g_i(x)\neq0,
\\[2ex]
\overline{\mathbb B}_{d_i},
&
g_i(x)=0.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Set $
\mathcal D(x)
:= \prod_{i=1}^N\mathcal D_i(x)\,,
F(x):=-\mathcal D(x).$\\

At zero-gradient blocks, the unit ball provides a closed convex
completion of the normalized field, while away from such blocks the
completion reduces to the unique normalized gradient direction. The
graph-consistency result below (Lemma~\ref{lem:graph-consistency-finite-resolution}) shows that this completion captures all
asymptotic cluster behavior of the finite-resolution comparison fields
as the comparison radius vanishes.
