We start by checking Assumption~\ref{as:set-val-mapping} regarding the regularity of the set-valued mapping $F$.

\begin{tcolorbox}[colframe=white!, top=2pt,left=2pt,right=2pt,bottom=2pt] 
\begin{lemma}[Regularity of the completed field]
\label{lem:completed-field-regularity}
The map
$F=-\mathcal D:\mathcal U\rightrightarrows\mathbb R^d$ defined above in (\ref{def:completed-potential-field})  
is upper hemicontinuous, locally bounded, and has nonempty compact
convex values. Moreover,
$\sup_{v\in\mathcal D(x)}\|v\|
\le\sqrt N.$
\end{lemma}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{proof}
Nonemptiness, compactness, convexity, and uniform boundedness are
immediate from the definition.

It remains to establish upper hemicontinuity. Since the values are
compact and the map is locally bounded, it suffices in finite
dimension to prove that its graph is closed.

Let $x_n\to x$ and $v_n\to v$ with
$v_n\in\mathcal D(x_n)$. Let $i \in [N]$. 
If $g_i(x)\neq0$, continuity of $g_i$ gives $g_i(x_n)\neq0$ for sufficiently large $n$, and hence  
\begin{equation*}
    v_{i,n}
=
\frac{g_i(x_n)}{\|g_i(x_n)\|}
\longrightarrow
\frac{g_i(x)}{\|g_i(x)\|}.
\end{equation*}
Thus $v_i\in\mathcal D_i(x)$.
If $g_i(x)=0$, then $\|v_{i,n}\|\le1$, so
$\|v_i\|\le1$ and $v_i\in\overline{\mathbb B}_{d_i}
=\mathcal D_i(x)$.
Thus $v\in\mathcal D(x)$ and the graph is closed.
\end{proof}

\begin{tcolorbox}[colframe=white!, top=2pt,left=2pt,right=2pt,bottom=2pt] 
\begin{lemma}[Graph consistency]
\label{lem:graph-consistency-finite-resolution}
As $\delta\downarrow0$,
\[
\sup_{x\in\mathcal X^\delta}
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
(x,D^\delta_\Phi(x)),
\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D)
\right)
\longrightarrow0\,,
\]
where
$\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D)
:= \{(x,v)\in \mathcal X\times\mathbb R^d:\ v\in\mathcal D(x)\}$ 
is the graph of the set-valued mapping $\mathcal D$.
\end{lemma}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{proof}
Suppose, towards a contradiction, that the graph-consistency statement
fails. Then there exist $\varepsilon>0$, a sequence
$\delta_n\downarrow0$, and $x_n\in\mathcal X^{\delta_n}$ such that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:contradiction-graph-cv}
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
(x_n,D_\Phi^{\delta_n}(x_n)),
\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D)
\right)
\ge\varepsilon,
\qquad
\forall n.
\end{equation}

Since $\mathcal X$ is compact, after passing to a subsequence we may
assume that
$x_n\to\bar x\in\mathcal X.$ 
Moreover, since
$\|D_\Phi^{\delta_n}(x_n)\|\le\sqrt N,$ 
the sequence $(D_\Phi^{\delta_n}(x_n))$ is bounded. After extracting
a further subsequence, we may therefore assume that
$D_\Phi^{\delta_n}(x_n)\to v$
for some
$v=(v_1,\ldots,v_N)\in\mathbb R^d.$

We claim that
$v\in\mathcal D(\bar x).$
To prove this, let $i\in[N]$. 

If $\nabla_i\Phi(\bar x)=0$, then
$\|D_i^{\delta_n}(x_n)\|\le1$
for all $n$. 
Passing to the limit gives
$\|v_i\|\le 1.$
Hence
$v_i\in\overline{\mathbb B}_{d_i}
=\mathcal D_i(\bar x).$

Suppose now that $\nabla_i\Phi(\bar x)\neq0$.
By continuity of $\nabla_i\Phi$,
$\nabla_i\Phi(x_n)\neq0$ for all sufficiently large $n$. Define
\[
q_n
:=
\frac{\nabla_i\Phi(x_n)}
{\|\nabla_i\Phi(x_n)\|}.
\]
By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform-potential-alignment-as},
\[
\left\langle
q_n,D_i^{\delta_n}(x_n)
\right\rangle
\ge
1-
\frac{L\delta_n}
{\kappa_{d_i}\|\nabla_i\Phi(x_n)\|}.
\]
Since $\|D_i^{\delta_n}(x_n)\|\le1$, we have
\begin{align*}
\left\|
D_i^{\delta_n}(x_n)-q_n
\right\|^2
&=
\|D_i^{\delta_n}(x_n)\|^2+1
-
2\left\langle
D_i^{\delta_n}(x_n),q_n
\right\rangle
\\
&\le
2\left(
1-
\left\langle
D_i^{\delta_n}(x_n),q_n
\right\rangle
\right)
\\
&\le
\frac{2L\delta_n}
{\kappa_{d_i}\|\nabla_i\Phi(x_n)\|}
\longrightarrow0 \,,
\end{align*}
where the above limit follows from noting that 
$\delta_n\to0$ while
$\|\nabla_i\Phi(x_n)\|
\longrightarrow
\|\nabla_i\Phi(\bar x)\|>0.$ 
Therefore,
$D_i^{\delta_n}(x_n)-q_n\to0.$ 
Since 
$q_n
\longrightarrow
\frac{\nabla_i\Phi(\bar x)}
{\|\nabla_i\Phi(\bar x)\|},$ 
and
$D_i^{\delta_n}(x_n)\to v_i,$ 
we conclude that
\[
v_i
=
\frac{\nabla_i\Phi(\bar x)}
{\|\nabla_i\Phi(\bar x)\|}
\in
\mathcal D_i(\bar x).
\]

Since $i\in[N]$ was arbitrary, we have shown that $v_i\in\mathcal D_i(\bar x)$ for all $i\in [N]$.
Hence $v\in \prod_{i=1}^N\mathcal D_i(\bar x)
=\mathcal D(\bar x),$ 
so
$(\bar x,v)\in\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D).$ 

Consequently,
\[
\operatorname{dist}\left(
(x_n,D_\Phi^{\delta_n}(x_n)),
\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D)
\right)
\le
\left\|
(x_n,D_\Phi^{\delta_n}(x_n))-(\bar x,v)
\right\|
\longrightarrow0,
\]
because
$x_n\to\bar x,
D_\Phi^{\delta_n}(x_n)\to v.$ 
This contradicts
(\ref{eq:contradiction-graph-cv}). Therefore, as $\delta\downarrow0$, 
\[
\sup_{x\in\mathcal X^\delta}
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
(x,D_\Phi^\delta(x)),
\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D)
\right)
\longrightarrow0\,.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{tcolorbox}[colframe=white!, top=2pt,left=2pt,right=2pt,bottom=2pt] 
\begin{lemma}[CNGP tracks the completed normalized inclusion]
\label{lem:cngp-ppad}
Under the assumptions of
Theorem~\ref{thm:as-comparison-ne}, there exist sequences
$y_t\in\mathcal X$, $v_t\in\mathcal D(y_t)$, $r_t\to0$, and a
bounded martingale-difference sequence $e_{t+1}$ such that, almost
surely,
\[
z_{t+1}
=
\Pi_{\mathcal X}
\left[
z_t+\eta_t(-v_t+e_{t+1}+r_t)
\right],
\qquad
\|y_t-z_t\|\to0,
\]
and
$\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\eta_t e_{t+1}$
converges. 
Consequently, $(z_t)$ is an $\mathcal X$-projected perturbed
approximate discretization of $F=-\mathcal D$ (in the sense of Theorem~\ref{thm:majewski-et-al4.2}). 
\end{lemma}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{proof}
Recall first that (\ref{eq:cngp}) can be written as follows: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:recall-algo-cngp}
z_{t+1} = \Pi_{\mathcal X}(z_t - \eta_t \hat{D}_t)\,.
\end{equation}
Define $e_{t+1} := - M_{t+1} = - (\widehat D_t
-
D_\Phi^{\delta_t}(x_t))$. We can then write: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:decomp1}
- \hat{D}_t = - (\hat{D}_t - D_{\Phi}^{\delta_t}(x_t)) - D_{\Phi}^{\delta_t}(x_t) = - D_{\Phi}^{\delta_t}(x_t) + e_{t+1}\,.
\end{equation}

Our next step is to decompose the term $- D_{\Phi}^{\delta_t}(x_t)$ using the graph consistency result (Lemma~\ref{lem:graph-consistency-finite-resolution}) relating it to our completed set-valued mapping $-\mathcal{D}$ defined in (\ref{def:completed-potential-field}).  

Define
\[
\varepsilon_t
:=
\sup_{x\in\mathcal X^{\delta_t}}
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
(x,D_\Phi^{\delta_t}(x)),
\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D)
\right).
\]
By Lemma~\ref{lem:graph-consistency-finite-resolution} and
$\delta_t\to0$, we have $\varepsilon_t\to0$. Since
$x_t\in\mathcal X^{\delta_t}$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:dist}
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
(x_t,D_\Phi^{\delta_t}(x_t)),
\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D)
\right)
\le \varepsilon_t.
\end{equation}
Since $\mathcal X$ is compact and
$\sup_{v\in\mathcal D(x)}\|v\|\le\sqrt N$, the closed graph
$\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D)$ is a compact subset of
$\mathcal X\times\mathbb R^d$. 
Hence, the distance in (\ref{eq:dist})
is attained and there exist $y_t\in\mathcal X$ and
$v_t\in\mathcal D(y_t)$ such that
\[
\left\|
(x_t,D_\Phi^{\delta_t}(x_t))-(y_t,v_t)
\right\|
=
\operatorname{dist}\left(
(x_t,D_\Phi^{\delta_t}(x_t)),
\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D)
\right)
\le\varepsilon_t.
\]
In particular,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:limits-r_t-y_t-x_t}
\|y_t-x_t\|\le\varepsilon_t \longrightarrow 0,
\qquad
\|v_t-D_\Phi^{\delta_t}(x_t)\|\le\varepsilon_t \longrightarrow 0.
\end{equation}

We define now $r_t := v_t - D_\Phi^{\delta_t}(x_t)$ and we use (\ref{eq:decomp1}) to obtain: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:decomp-hatDt}
- \hat{D}_t = - D_{\Phi}^{\delta_t}(x_t) + e_{t+1} 
= - v_t + e_{t+1} + r_t\,, \quad v_t \in \mathcal{D}(y_t)\,.
\end{equation}
Therefore, using (\ref{eq:recall-algo-cngp}) and the above decomposition~(\ref{eq:decomp-hatDt}), we obtain: 
\[
z_{t+1}
=
\Pi_{\mathcal X}
\left[
z_t+
\eta_t
\bigl(
f_t + e_{t+1} + r_t
\bigr)
\right]\,,
\]
where $f_t := - v_t \in F(y_t) = - \mathcal{D}(y_t), e_{t+1} = - M_{t+1} = - (\widehat D_t
-
D_\Phi^{\delta_t}(x_t)), r_t = v_t - D_{\Phi}^{\delta_t}(x_t)$. 

We now check that the assumptions \ref{as:set-val-mapping} to \ref{as:bounded-error} in Theorem~\ref{thm:majewski-et-al4.2} are satisfied. 

Assumption~\ref{as:set-val-mapping} is satisfied by Lemma~\ref{lem:completed-field-regularity}. 

Assumption~\ref{as:stepsize} follows from
Assumption~\ref{as:rm-stepsizes}; indeed
$\sum_t\eta_t^2<\infty$ implies $\eta_t\to0$.

Assumption~\ref{as:bias-and-stoch-error}: 
Since the comparison field of the game and that of the potential
coincide exactly at finite resolution,
Lemma~\ref{lem:one-bit-estimator} gives
$\mathbb E[M_{t+1}\mid\mathcal F_t]=0.$

Moreover, the single-comparison estimator is uniformly bounded:
$
\|\widehat D_t\|
\le
G:=
\left(
\sum_{i=1}^N G_i^2
\right)^{1/2}
$ almost surely. 
Consequently,
$\|M_{t+1}\|\le \|\hat{D}_t\| + \|D_{\Phi}^{\delta_t}(x_t)\| \leq G + \sqrt{N}$ almost surely. Therefore, 
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{t=1}^{+\infty} \mathbb{E}[\|\eta_tM_{t+1}\|^2|\mathcal{F}_t] \leq (G + \sqrt{N})^2 \sum_{t=1}^{+\infty} \eta_t^2 < \infty. 
\end{equation*}
Since $\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\eta_t^2<\infty$ by Assumption~\ref{as:rm-stepsizes}, 
the vector-valued martingale series 
$\sum_{t=1}^T\eta_t M_{t+1}$ 
converges almost surely. Noting that $e_{t+1} = - M_{t+1}$, it follows that
$\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\eta_t e_{t+1}$ 
converges almost surely, as required by
Assumption~\ref{as:bias-and-stoch-error}. 
We henceforth fix a sample path in the probability-one event on
which the preceding martingale series converges and the uniform
bounds hold. 

Recall from (\ref{eq:limits-r_t-y_t-x_t}) that $r_t\to0$.  
Furthermore, recall that by construction,
$x_{i,t}=
\mathsf Q_{i,\delta_t}(z_{i,t}),$ 
and hence
$\|x_{i,t}-z_{i,t}\|
\le
\frac{R_i}{\rho_i}\delta_t.$ 
Therefore, with
$C_{\mathcal X}
:=
\left(
\sum_{i=1}^N
\frac{R_i^2}{\rho_i^2}
\right)^{1/2}$, 
we have
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:x-z-asymptotic2}
\|x_t-z_t\|
\le
C_{\mathcal X}\delta_t
\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
Hence, we obtain using (\ref{eq:limits-r_t-y_t-x_t}) and (\ref{eq:x-z-asymptotic2}), 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:state-y-to-zt-below}
\|y_t-z_t\|
\le
\|y_t-x_t\|+\|x_t-z_t\| \le \varepsilon_t + C_{\mathcal{X}} \delta_t
\longrightarrow0\,,
\end{equation}
where the limit follows from using $\varepsilon_t \to 0, \delta_t \to 0$. 
Thus Assumptions~\ref{as:bias-and-stoch-error} and
\ref{as:state-y-to-zt} are satisfied.

Assumption~\ref{as:bounded-error} also holds as we have shown above that 
$\sup_t\|M_{t+1}\|<\infty$ almost surely. 
\end{proof}

\noindent\textbf{Lyapunov analysis of the projected limiting inclusion.} 
We now verify the Lyapunov condition required by
\citet[Theorem~4.2]{majewski-miasojedow-moulines18}.
Recall that $F(x)=-\mathcal D(x)$ where $\mathcal{D}$ is defined in (\ref{def:completed-potential-field}). 

The projected limiting field on $\mathcal X$ is
\[
F_{\mathcal X}(x)
:=
\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X}(x)}F(x).
\]
Since
$\mathcal X=\prod_{i=1}^N\mathcal X_i,$ 
we have
$T_{\mathcal X}(x)
=\prod_{i=1}^N T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)$, 
and Euclidean projection onto the tangent cone acts blockwise.

We next show that the ordinal potential $\Phi$ acts as a Lyapunov function for the projected differential inclusion induced by the (\ref{eq:cngp}) stochastic approximation algorithm, i.e. we verify the second part in Theorem~\ref{thm:majewski-et-al4.2}, namely (\ref{eq:majewski-specialized-Lyapunov}) and the identification of the zero set of the upper-bounding function $U$. 

\begin{tcolorbox}[colframe=white!, top=2pt,left=2pt,right=2pt,bottom=2pt] 
\begin{lemma}[Lyapunov inequality for the projected normalized field]
\label{lem:lyapunov-ineq}
For every $i\in[N]$ define
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:psi_i}
\Psi_i(x)
:=
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle
\|g_i(x)\| \cdot 
\left\|
\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}
\left(
-\frac{g_i(x)}{\|g_i(x)\|}
\right)
\right\|^2,
&
\text{if} \,\,g_i(x)\neq0,
\\[3ex]
0,
&
\text{if} \,\, g_i(x)=0,
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
and let $U(x)
:=
-\sum_{i=1}^N\Psi_i(x).$ 
Then $U:\mathcal X\to\mathbb R$ is upper semicontinuous on $\mathcal X$, $U\le0$, and for
every $x\in\mathcal X$ and every $u\in F(x)$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:lyap-ineq}
\left\langle
\nabla\Phi(x),
\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X}(x)}u
\right\rangle
=
U(x).
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:identification-zero-set}
\{x\in\mathcal X:U(x)=0\}
=
\left\{
x\in\mathcal X:
-\nabla\Phi(x)\in N_{\mathcal X}(x)
\right\}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{proof}

\noindent\textbf{Proof of (\ref{eq:lyap-ineq}).}
Let $x\in\mathcal X$ and $w\in F_{\mathcal X}(x).$ 
By definition of the projected field, there exists
$u\in F(x)=-\mathcal D(x)$ such that
$w=\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X}(x)}(u).$ 
Since both the action set and tangent cone are Cartesian products,
this means blockwise that
$w_i =\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}(u_i),
i\in[N].$ 

Observe first that 
\begin{equation*}
\langle\nabla\Phi(x),w\rangle
= \sum_{i=1}^N \langle g_i(x), w_i\rangle\,.
\end{equation*}
To prove (\ref{eq:lyap-ineq}), we will show in what follows that for every $i \in [N]$, 
\begin{equation}
 \langle g_i(x), w_i\rangle = - \Psi_i(x)\,.
\end{equation}

Consider first a block $i \in [N]$ such that $g_i(x)\neq0$.
Then $F_i(x)$ is a singleton and necessarily
$u_i=-\frac{g_i(x)}{\|g_i(x)\|}.$ 

For a cone $C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_i}$, let
$C^\circ := \{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d_i} :
\langle v,w\rangle \leq 0,\ \forall w\in C\}$ 
denote its polar cone. For a closed convex cone $C$, Moreau's decomposition gives
\[
u_i=\Pi_C(u_i)+\Pi_{C^\circ}(u_i),
\qquad
\langle \Pi_C(u_i),\Pi_{C^\circ}(u_i)\rangle=0.
\]
Taking
$C=T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i), C^\circ=N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)$, 
and recalling that $w_i=\Pi_C(u_i)$, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
\langle u_i,w_i\rangle = \langle \Pi_C(u_i)+\Pi_{C^\circ}(u_i),\Pi_C(u_i)\rangle  
= \|\Pi_C(u_i)\|^2 = \|w_i\|^2\,.
\end{equation*}
Since $g_i(x)=-\|g_i(x)\|u_i$, 
it follows that
\begin{align}
\langle g_i(x),w_i\rangle
&=
-\|g_i(x)\|
\langle u_i,w_i\rangle
\nonumber\\
&=
-\|g_i(x)\|\cdot\|w_i\|^2
\nonumber\\
&=
-\|g_i(x)\| \cdot 
\left\|
\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}
\left(
-\frac{g_i(x)}{\|g_i(x)\|}
\right)
\right\|^2
\nonumber\\
&=
-\Psi_i(x).
\label{eq:block-lie-dissipation}
\end{align}

If instead $g_i(x)=0$, the block $u_i$ may be any element of
$-\overline{\mathbb B}_{d_i}$, but independently of this choice,
\[
\langle g_i(x),w_i\rangle=0=-\Psi_i(x).
\]
Summing over the blocks gives
\[
\langle\nabla\Phi(x),w\rangle
=
-\sum_{i=1}^N\Psi_i(x)
=
U(x).
\]
The right-hand side is independent of the particular selection
$u\in F(x)$ at zero-gradient blocks. Consequently, 
$\left\langle
\nabla\Phi(x),
\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X}(x)}u
\right\rangle
=
U(x).$ Since $\Psi_i(x)\ge0$ for every $i$, we also have $U(x)\le0$.\\

\noindent\textbf{Proof of upper semicontinuity of $U$.}
Since $U=-\sum_{i=1}^N\Psi_i$, it suffices to show that each $\Psi_i$ is lower semicontinuous to conclude that their negative sum is upper semicontinuous, i.e., for every $x^n \to x$, 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:psi-i-lower-semicont}
\Psi_i(x) \leq \liminf_n \Psi_i(x^n)\,.
\end{equation}

First, by definition, $\Psi_i(y) \geq 0$ for every $y \in \mathcal{X}$. If $g_i(x) = 0$, then $\Psi_i(x) = 0$ by definition (see (\ref{eq:psi_i})). Therefore for any such $x$ for which $g_i(x) = 0$, (\ref{eq:psi-i-lower-semicont}) holds since $0 = \Psi_i(x) \leq \liminf_n \Psi_i(x^n)\,.$ 

It remains to show lower semicontinuity of $\Psi_i$ whenever $g_i(x)\neq0$. Positive homogeneity of the projection onto a cone yields: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:alternative-expression-psi-i}
\Psi_i(x)
= \|g_i(x)\| \cdot 
\left\|
\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}
\left(
-\frac{g_i(x)}{\|g_i(x)\|}
\right)
\right\|^2
= \frac{\left\|
\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}
\left(
-g_i(x)
\right)
\right\|^2}{\|g_i(x)\|}\,.
\end{equation}
Using Moreau's decomposition gives: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:dist-to-normal-cone}
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
-g_i(x),N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)
\right) = \left\| - g_i(x) - \Pi_{\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}(- g_i(x)) \right\|
= \left\|\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}
\left(
-g_i(x)
\right)\right\|\,.
\end{equation}
Combining (\ref{eq:alternative-expression-psi-i}) and (\ref{eq:dist-to-normal-cone}) yields: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:Psi-normal-distance}
\Psi_i(x)
=
\frac{
\operatorname{dist}^2
\left(
-g_i(x),N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)
\right)
}{
\|g_i(x)\|
}.
\end{equation}
We now use this expression to show that $\Psi_i$ is lower semicontinuous in the case where $g_i(x) \neq 0$. Noting that $g_i$ is continuous, our main task is to show that the function $d_i: \mathcal{X} \to \mathbb{R}_+$ defined for any $x \in \mathcal{X}$ by:  
\[
d_i(x)
:=
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
-g_i(x),N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)
\right)
\]
is lower semicontinuous. Note here, that this is not a simple distance function to a fixed convex set which is known to be continuous. Here the set $\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{X}_i}(x_i)$ also depends on the variable $x_i.$ We prove the lower semicontinuity claim below. 

Let $x^n\to x$ and, after passing to
a subsequence realizing $\liminf_n d_i(x^n)$, let 
\[
v_i^n
:=
\Pi_{N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i^n)}
\bigl(-g_i(x^n)\bigr).
\]
Since $0\in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i^n)$,
$\|v_i^n\|\le\|g_i(x^n)\|$ by nonexpansiveness of the projection operator. As a consequence, since $(g(x^n))$ converges by continuity of $g$, it follows that it is bounded and hence that $(v_i^n)$ is also bounded. 
We can then pass to a further subsequence and let $v_i^n\to v_i$. 
The normal-cone mapping of a closed convex set has closed graph, and since $v_i^n \in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i^n)$, we obtain  
$v_i\in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)$. Therefore,
\[
d_i(x)
= \inf_{y_i \in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)} \|- g_i(x) - y_i\|
\le
\|-g_i(x)-v_i\|
= \lim_{n \to \infty} \|-g_i(x^n) - v_i^n\| 
=
\liminf_{n\to\infty} d_i(x^n)\,,
\]
where the penultimate identity follows from continuity of $g_i$ and $x^n \to x, v_i^n \to v_i$, and the last identity follows from recalling that $x^n$ is a subsequence realizing $\liminf_n d_i(x^n)$. 
We have proved that $d_i$ is lower semicontinuous. 

We now conclude to show that $\Psi_i$ as expressed in (\ref{eq:Psi-normal-distance}) is lower semicontinuous. 
We use the following elementary fact: 

\begin{lemma}
\label{lem:liminf}
If $a_n\geq 0$ and $b_n\to b>0$, then
$\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{b_n}
=\frac{\liminf_{n\to\infty}a_n}{b}.$ 
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $\liminf_n a_n=+\infty$, the claim follows immediately from
$b_n\to b>0$. Hence assume that $\liminf_n a_n<+\infty$. 
We prove the two inequalities separately.

For the first inequality, let $(a_{n_k})$ be a subsequence such that
$a_{n_k}\to \liminf_{n\to\infty}a_n.$ 
Since $b_{n_k}\to b>0$, we have
$\frac{a_{n_k}}{b_{n_k}}
\longrightarrow
\frac{\liminf_{n\to\infty}a_n}{b}.$ 
Since the liminf of a sequence is no larger than any of its
subsequential limits,
\[
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{b_n}
\le
\frac{\liminf_{n\to\infty}a_n}{b}.
\]

For the reverse inequality, let
$\bigl(a_{n_k}/b_{n_k}\bigr)$ be a subsequence such that
$\frac{a_{n_k}}{b_{n_k}}
\longrightarrow
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{b_n}.$
Since $b_{n_k}\to b>0$,
\[
a_{n_k}
=
b_{n_k}\frac{a_{n_k}}{b_{n_k}}
\longrightarrow
b\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{b_n}.
\]
Again using that the liminf is no larger than any subsequential limit,
\[
\liminf_{n\to\infty}a_n
\le
b\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{b_n}.
\]
Since $b>0$,
$\frac{\liminf_{n\to\infty}a_n}{b}
\le
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{b_n}.$ 
Combining the two inequalities proves the claim.
\end{proof}

Take now $a_n = d_i(x^n)^2, b_n = \|g_i(x^n)\|$. Since $g_i(x) \neq 0$ and $g_i$ is continuous, it follows that $b_n \to \|g_i(x)\| > 0$. By lower semicontinuity of $d_i$, $d_i(x)^2 \leq (\liminf_{n \to \infty} d_i(x^n))^2 = \liminf_{n \to \infty} d_i(x^n)^2\,.$ Therefore, we can conclude using Lemma~\ref{lem:liminf}: 
\begin{equation}
\Psi_i(x) = \frac{d_i(x)^2}{\|g_i(x)\|} \leq \frac{\liminf_{n \to \infty} d_i(x^n)^2}{\|g_i(x)\|} 
= \liminf_{n \to \infty} \frac{d_i(x^n)^2}{\|g_i(x^n)\|} =  \liminf_{n \to \infty} \Psi_i(x^n)\,,
\end{equation}
which shows that $\Psi_i$ is lower semicontinuous.
Consequently, $U=-\sum_{i=1}^N\Psi_i$ is upper semicontinuous.\\ 

\noindent\textbf{Proof of (\ref{eq:identification-zero-set}): zeros of the Lyapunov upper bound.} Since each $\Psi_i(x)\ge0$,
$U(x)=0$ if and only if $\Psi_i(x)=0$ for all $i \in [N].$

If $g_i(x)=0$, then $-g_i(x)=0\in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)$ 
and $\Psi_i(x)=0$ by definition.

Suppose now that $g_i(x)\neq0$. Then
$\Psi_i(x)=0$ 
if and only if
$
\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}
\left(
-\frac{g_i(x)}{\|g_i(x)\|}
\right)
=0.$

For a closed convex cone $C$,
$\Pi_C(v)=0$ if and only if $v\in C^\circ$.
Since $T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)^\circ
= N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)$, 
we obtain
$\Psi_i(x)=0$ if and only if $-\frac{g_i(x)}{\|g_i(x)\|}
\in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i).$ 
Since the normal cone is a cone, this is equivalent to
$-g_i(x)\in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i).$ 
This proves (\ref{eq:identification-zero-set}). 
The product-space characterization follows from noting that $N_{\mathcal X}(x)
=\prod_{i=1}^N N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i).$ 
\end{proof}

For compatibility with Theorem~\ref{thm:majewski-et-al4.2}, we
extend $U$ from $\mathcal X$ to the ambient open set $\mathcal U$.
Define
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:extension-U}
\widetilde U(x)
:=
U\bigl(\Pi_{\mathcal X}(x)\bigr),
\qquad x\in\mathcal U.
\end{equation}
Since $\mathcal X$ is nonempty, closed, and convex,
$\Pi_{\mathcal X}$ is continuous. Hence $\widetilde U$ is upper
semicontinuous on $\mathcal U$ as the composition of the upper
semicontinuous function $U$ with the continuous map
$\Pi_{\mathcal X}$. Moreover,
$\widetilde U(x)=U(x), 
\forall x\in\mathcal X,$ 
and $\widetilde U\le0$ on $\mathcal U$.
With a slight abuse of notation, we henceforth denote this extension
again by $U$.

\begin{tcolorbox}[colframe=white!, top=2pt,left=2pt,right=2pt,bottom=2pt] 
\begin{lemma}[Potential stationarity is equivalent to Nash equilibrium]
\label{lem:potential-stationarity-ne}
Under Assumption~\ref{ass:block-pseudoconvex} and
Assumption~\ref{as:non-degeneracy-ord-pot},
$\mathcal S = \operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G),$ 
where $\mathcal S=\{x\in\mathcal X:U(x)=0\}$.
\end{lemma}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{proof}
By (\ref{eq:identification-zero-set}) in Lemma~\ref{lem:lyapunov-ineq} ,
$x\in\mathcal S$ if and only if 
$-\nabla_i\Phi(x) \in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i), 
\forall i\in[N].$

We first prove that $\mathcal S\subseteq\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)$. Let $x\in\mathcal S$, $i \in [N]$. 

If $\nabla_i\Phi(x)=0,$
Assumption~\ref{as:non-degeneracy-ord-pot} gives
$\nabla_iJ_i(x)=0.$ 
Suppose for contradiction that $x_i$ is not a best response to
$x_{-i}$. Then there exists $y_i\in\mathcal X_i$ such that
$J_i(y_i,x_{-i}) < J_i(x_i,x_{-i}).$ 
By blockwise pseudoconvexity,
$\left\langle
\nabla_iJ_i(x),
y_i-x_i
\right\rangle
<0,$ contradicting $\nabla_iJ_i(x)=0$.
Thus $x_i$ is a best response.

Suppose now that
$\nabla_i\Phi(x)\neq0.$ 
By Lemma~\ref{lem:ordinal-potential-gradient-alignment},
$\nabla_iJ_i(x) =
\lambda_i(x)\nabla_i\Phi(x)$ 
for some $\lambda_i(x)>0$.
Since
$-\nabla_i\Phi(x) \in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)$ 
and the normal cone is a cone,
\[
-\nabla_iJ_i(x)
=
-\lambda_i(x)\nabla_i\Phi(x)
\in
N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i).
\]
Equivalently,
$\left\langle
\nabla_iJ_i(x),
y_i-x_i
\right\rangle
\ge0, 
\forall y_i\in\mathcal X_i.$ 
If there were $y_i\in\mathcal X_i$ satisfying
\[
J_i(y_i,x_{-i})
<
J_i(x_i,x_{-i}),
\]
blockwise pseudoconvexity would imply
$\left\langle
\nabla_iJ_i(x),
y_i-x_i
\right\rangle
<0,$ 
a contradiction. Hence $x_i$ is a best response.
Since this holds for every $i \in [N]$,
$x\in\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G).$

Conversely, suppose
$x\in\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G).$ 
For every $i \in [N]$, $x_i$ minimizes the differentiable
function
$y_i\mapsto J_i(y_i,x_{-i})$ 
over the convex set $\mathcal X_i$. The first-order necessary
optimality condition therefore gives
$\left\langle
\nabla_iJ_i(x),
y_i-x_i
\right\rangle
\ge0, 
\forall y_i\in\mathcal X_i,$
or equivalently, 
$-\nabla_iJ_i(x)
\in
N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i).$ 

If $\nabla_iJ_i(x)=0$, nondegeneracy gives
$\nabla_i\Phi(x)=0$, and consequently
$-\nabla_i\Phi(x)=0 \in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i).$ 

If $\nabla_iJ_i(x)\neq0$, Lemma
~\ref{lem:ordinal-potential-gradient-alignment} gives
$\nabla_iJ_i(x) = \lambda_i(x)\nabla_i\Phi(x),
\lambda_i(x)>0.$ 
Hence
\[
-\nabla_i\Phi(x)
=
\frac1{\lambda_i(x)}
\bigl(-\nabla_iJ_i(x)\bigr)
\in
N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i).
\]
Thus the potential stationarity condition holds for every player,
so
$x\in\mathcal S.$
This proves 
$\mathcal S
=
\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G).$
\end{proof}

\noindent\textbf{End of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:as-comparison-ne}.} 
Applying Theorem~\ref{thm:majewski-et-al4.2} with
$\mathcal K=\mathcal X,
F=-\mathcal D,
V=\Phi$, 
and with the upper semicontinuous extension of $U$ defined in
(\ref{eq:extension-U}), the Lyapunov condition
(\ref{eq:majewski-specialized-Lyapunov}) follows from
Lemma~\ref{lem:lyapunov-ineq}, since this extension agrees with the
original $U$ on $\mathcal X$.
Using $\mathcal S=\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)$ (see Lemma~\ref{lem:potential-stationarity-ne}) 
and $\operatorname{int}_{\mathbb R}\Phi(\mathcal S)
=\operatorname{int}_{\mathbb R}\Phi(\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G))=\varnothing$ by assumption, 
we obtain, almost surely,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:as-cv-NE}
\operatorname{dist}
\bigl(
z_t,\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)
\bigr)
\longrightarrow0\,, \quad 
\Phi(z_t)\longrightarrow\Phi_\infty\,, 
\end{equation}
for some finite random variable $\Phi_\infty$.

We now transfer this conclusion to the sequence $(x_t)$. By construction,
$x_{i,t}=
\mathsf Q_{i,\delta_t}(z_{i,t}),$ 
and hence
$\|x_{i,t}-z_{i,t}\|
\le
\frac{R_i}{\rho_i}\delta_t.$ 
Therefore, with
$C_{\mathcal X}
:=
\left(
\sum_{i=1}^N
\frac{R_i^2}{\rho_i^2}
\right)^{1/2}$, 
we have
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:x-z-asymptotic}
\|x_t-z_t\|
\le
C_{\mathcal X}\delta_t
\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
Combining
(\ref{eq:as-cv-NE}) and
(\ref{eq:x-z-asymptotic}),
\begin{align*}
\operatorname{dist}
\bigl(
x_t,
\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)
\bigr)
&\le
\operatorname{dist}
\bigl(
z_t,
\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)
\bigr)
+
\|x_t-z_t\| \underset{t \to \infty}{\longrightarrow} 0\,. 
\end{align*}
Moreover, uniform continuity of $\Phi$ on the compact set
$\mathcal X$ yields
$|\Phi(x_t)-\Phi(z_t)|\longrightarrow0,$ 
and hence 
$\Phi(x_t)\longrightarrow\Phi_\infty.$

Suppose
$\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G) =\{x_\star^1,\ldots,x_\star^m\}$ 
is finite. The rest of the proof is standard. For $m\ge2$ let
$\Delta
:=
\min_{j\neq k}\|x_\star^j-x_\star^k\|>0.$ 
Since
$\operatorname{dist}(z_t,\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G))\to0,$
eventually
\[
z_t\in
\bigcup_{j=1}^m
B(x_\star^j,\Delta/4).
\]
On the other hand, nonexpansiveness of projection and the uniform bound
$\|\widehat D_t\|\le G$ give
\[
\|z_{t+1}-z_t\|
\le
G\eta_t
\longrightarrow0.
\]
Hence, eventually,
$\|z_{t+1}-z_t\|<\Delta/2.$ 
Since distinct balls
$B(x_\star^j,\Delta/4)$ are separated by distance at least
$\Delta/2$, while eventually
$\|z_{t+1}-z_t\|<\Delta/2$, the sequence cannot move between
two distinct such balls after some finite time, and remains in only one such
ball.  Together with
$\operatorname{dist}(z_t,\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G))\to0$, this
implies
\[
z_t\to X_\infty
\]
for some random variable $X_\infty\in\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)$. Finally,
$\|x_t-z_t\|\to0$ gives $x_t\to X_\infty.$ 

If $\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)$ is a singleton, the same
conclusion follows immediately from
$\operatorname{dist}
\bigl(
x_t,\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)
\bigr)
\longrightarrow0.$ 
This completes the proof.
<!-- end head-4 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:completed-field-regularity}
The map
$F=-\mathcal D:\mathcal U\rightrightarrows\mathbb R^d$ defined above in (\ref{def:completed-potential-field})  
is upper hemicontinuous, locally bounded, and has nonempty compact
convex values. Moreover,
$\sup_{v\in\mathcal D(x)}\|v\|
\le\sqrt N.$
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Nonemptiness, compactness, convexity, and uniform boundedness are
immediate from the definition.

It remains to establish upper hemicontinuity. Since the values are
compact and the map is locally bounded, it suffices in finite
dimension to prove that its graph is closed.

Let $x_n\to x$ and $v_n\to v$ with
$v_n\in\mathcal D(x_n)$. Let $i \in [N]$. 
If $g_i(x)\neq0$, continuity of $g_i$ gives $g_i(x_n)\neq0$ for sufficiently large $n$, and hence  
\begin{equation*}
    v_{i,n}
=
\frac{g_i(x_n)}{\|g_i(x_n)\|}
\longrightarrow
\frac{g_i(x)}{\|g_i(x)\|}.
\end{equation*}
Thus $v_i\in\mathcal D_i(x)$.
If $g_i(x)=0$, then $\|v_{i,n}\|\le1$, so
$\|v_i\|\le1$ and $v_i\in\overline{\mathbb B}_{d_i}
=\mathcal D_i(x)$.
Thus $v\in\mathcal D(x)$ and the graph is closed.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:graph-consistency-finite-resolution}
As $\delta\downarrow0$,
\[
\sup_{x\in\mathcal X^\delta}
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
(x,D^\delta_\Phi(x)),
\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D)
\right)
\longrightarrow0\,,
\]
where
$\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D)
:= \{(x,v)\in \mathcal X\times\mathbb R^d:\ v\in\mathcal D(x)\}$ 
is the graph of the set-valued mapping $\mathcal D$.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Suppose, towards a contradiction, that the graph-consistency statement
fails. Then there exist $\varepsilon>0$, a sequence
$\delta_n\downarrow0$, and $x_n\in\mathcal X^{\delta_n}$ such that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:contradiction-graph-cv}
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
(x_n,D_\Phi^{\delta_n}(x_n)),
\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D)
\right)
\ge\varepsilon,
\qquad
\forall n.
\end{equation}

Since $\mathcal X$ is compact, after passing to a subsequence we may
assume that
$x_n\to\bar x\in\mathcal X.$ 
Moreover, since
$\|D_\Phi^{\delta_n}(x_n)\|\le\sqrt N,$ 
the sequence $(D_\Phi^{\delta_n}(x_n))$ is bounded. After extracting
a further subsequence, we may therefore assume that
$D_\Phi^{\delta_n}(x_n)\to v$
for some
$v=(v_1,\ldots,v_N)\in\mathbb R^d.$

We claim that
$v\in\mathcal D(\bar x).$
To prove this, let $i\in[N]$. 

If $\nabla_i\Phi(\bar x)=0$, then
$\|D_i^{\delta_n}(x_n)\|\le1$
for all $n$. 
Passing to the limit gives
$\|v_i\|\le 1.$
Hence
$v_i\in\overline{\mathbb B}_{d_i}
=\mathcal D_i(\bar x).$

Suppose now that $\nabla_i\Phi(\bar x)\neq0$.
By continuity of $\nabla_i\Phi$,
$\nabla_i\Phi(x_n)\neq0$ for all sufficiently large $n$. Define
\[
q_n
:=
\frac{\nabla_i\Phi(x_n)}
{\|\nabla_i\Phi(x_n)\|}.
\]
By Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform-potential-alignment-as},
\[
\left\langle
q_n,D_i^{\delta_n}(x_n)
\right\rangle
\ge
1-
\frac{L\delta_n}
{\kappa_{d_i}\|\nabla_i\Phi(x_n)\|}.
\]
Since $\|D_i^{\delta_n}(x_n)\|\le1$, we have
\begin{align*}
\left\|
D_i^{\delta_n}(x_n)-q_n
\right\|^2
&=
\|D_i^{\delta_n}(x_n)\|^2+1
-
2\left\langle
D_i^{\delta_n}(x_n),q_n
\right\rangle
\\
&\le
2\left(
1-
\left\langle
D_i^{\delta_n}(x_n),q_n
\right\rangle
\right)
\\
&\le
\frac{2L\delta_n}
{\kappa_{d_i}\|\nabla_i\Phi(x_n)\|}
\longrightarrow0 \,,
\end{align*}
where the above limit follows from noting that 
$\delta_n\to0$ while
$\|\nabla_i\Phi(x_n)\|
\longrightarrow
\|\nabla_i\Phi(\bar x)\|>0.$ 
Therefore,
$D_i^{\delta_n}(x_n)-q_n\to0.$ 
Since 
$q_n
\longrightarrow
\frac{\nabla_i\Phi(\bar x)}
{\|\nabla_i\Phi(\bar x)\|},$ 
and
$D_i^{\delta_n}(x_n)\to v_i,$ 
we conclude that
\[
v_i
=
\frac{\nabla_i\Phi(\bar x)}
{\|\nabla_i\Phi(\bar x)\|}
\in
\mathcal D_i(\bar x).
\]

Since $i\in[N]$ was arbitrary, we have shown that $v_i\in\mathcal D_i(\bar x)$ for all $i\in [N]$.
Hence $v\in \prod_{i=1}^N\mathcal D_i(\bar x)
=\mathcal D(\bar x),$ 
so
$(\bar x,v)\in\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D).$ 

Consequently,
\[
\operatorname{dist}\left(
(x_n,D_\Phi^{\delta_n}(x_n)),
\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D)
\right)
\le
\left\|
(x_n,D_\Phi^{\delta_n}(x_n))-(\bar x,v)
\right\|
\longrightarrow0,
\]
because
$x_n\to\bar x,
D_\Phi^{\delta_n}(x_n)\to v.$ 
This contradicts
(\ref{eq:contradiction-graph-cv}). Therefore, as $\delta\downarrow0$, 
\[
\sup_{x\in\mathcal X^\delta}
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
(x,D_\Phi^\delta(x)),
\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D)
\right)
\longrightarrow0\,.
\]
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:cngp-ppad}
Under the assumptions of
Theorem~\ref{thm:as-comparison-ne}, there exist sequences
$y_t\in\mathcal X$, $v_t\in\mathcal D(y_t)$, $r_t\to0$, and a
bounded martingale-difference sequence $e_{t+1}$ such that, almost
surely,
\[
z_{t+1}
=
\Pi_{\mathcal X}
\left[
z_t+\eta_t(-v_t+e_{t+1}+r_t)
\right],
\qquad
\|y_t-z_t\|\to0,
\]
and
$\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\eta_t e_{t+1}$
converges. 
Consequently, $(z_t)$ is an $\mathcal X$-projected perturbed
approximate discretization of $F=-\mathcal D$ (in the sense of Theorem~\ref{thm:majewski-et-al4.2}).
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Recall first that (\ref{eq:cngp}) can be written as follows: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:recall-algo-cngp}
z_{t+1} = \Pi_{\mathcal X}(z_t - \eta_t \hat{D}_t)\,.
\end{equation}
Define $e_{t+1} := - M_{t+1} = - (\widehat D_t
-
D_\Phi^{\delta_t}(x_t))$. We can then write: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:decomp1}
- \hat{D}_t = - (\hat{D}_t - D_{\Phi}^{\delta_t}(x_t)) - D_{\Phi}^{\delta_t}(x_t) = - D_{\Phi}^{\delta_t}(x_t) + e_{t+1}\,.
\end{equation}

Our next step is to decompose the term $- D_{\Phi}^{\delta_t}(x_t)$ using the graph consistency result (Lemma~\ref{lem:graph-consistency-finite-resolution}) relating it to our completed set-valued mapping $-\mathcal{D}$ defined in (\ref{def:completed-potential-field}).  

Define
\[
\varepsilon_t
:=
\sup_{x\in\mathcal X^{\delta_t}}
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
(x,D_\Phi^{\delta_t}(x)),
\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D)
\right).
\]
By Lemma~\ref{lem:graph-consistency-finite-resolution} and
$\delta_t\to0$, we have $\varepsilon_t\to0$. Since
$x_t\in\mathcal X^{\delta_t}$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:dist}
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
(x_t,D_\Phi^{\delta_t}(x_t)),
\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D)
\right)
\le \varepsilon_t.
\end{equation}
Since $\mathcal X$ is compact and
$\sup_{v\in\mathcal D(x)}\|v\|\le\sqrt N$, the closed graph
$\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D)$ is a compact subset of
$\mathcal X\times\mathbb R^d$. 
Hence, the distance in (\ref{eq:dist})
is attained and there exist $y_t\in\mathcal X$ and
$v_t\in\mathcal D(y_t)$ such that
\[
\left\|
(x_t,D_\Phi^{\delta_t}(x_t))-(y_t,v_t)
\right\|
=
\operatorname{dist}\left(
(x_t,D_\Phi^{\delta_t}(x_t)),
\operatorname{gph}(\mathcal D)
\right)
\le\varepsilon_t.
\]
In particular,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:limits-r_t-y_t-x_t}
\|y_t-x_t\|\le\varepsilon_t \longrightarrow 0,
\qquad
\|v_t-D_\Phi^{\delta_t}(x_t)\|\le\varepsilon_t \longrightarrow 0.
\end{equation}

We define now $r_t := v_t - D_\Phi^{\delta_t}(x_t)$ and we use (\ref{eq:decomp1}) to obtain: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:decomp-hatDt}
- \hat{D}_t = - D_{\Phi}^{\delta_t}(x_t) + e_{t+1} 
= - v_t + e_{t+1} + r_t\,, \quad v_t \in \mathcal{D}(y_t)\,.
\end{equation}
Therefore, using (\ref{eq:recall-algo-cngp}) and the above decomposition~(\ref{eq:decomp-hatDt}), we obtain: 
\[
z_{t+1}
=
\Pi_{\mathcal X}
\left[
z_t+
\eta_t
\bigl(
f_t + e_{t+1} + r_t
\bigr)
\right]\,,
\]
where $f_t := - v_t \in F(y_t) = - \mathcal{D}(y_t), e_{t+1} = - M_{t+1} = - (\widehat D_t
-
D_\Phi^{\delta_t}(x_t)), r_t = v_t - D_{\Phi}^{\delta_t}(x_t)$. 

We now check that the assumptions \ref{as:set-val-mapping} to \ref{as:bounded-error} in Theorem~\ref{thm:majewski-et-al4.2} are satisfied. 

Assumption~\ref{as:set-val-mapping} is satisfied by Lemma~\ref{lem:completed-field-regularity}. 

Assumption~\ref{as:stepsize} follows from
Assumption~\ref{as:rm-stepsizes}; indeed
$\sum_t\eta_t^2<\infty$ implies $\eta_t\to0$.

Assumption~\ref{as:bias-and-stoch-error}: 
Since the comparison field of the game and that of the potential
coincide exactly at finite resolution,
Lemma~\ref{lem:one-bit-estimator} gives
$\mathbb E[M_{t+1}\mid\mathcal F_t]=0.$

Moreover, the single-comparison estimator is uniformly bounded:
$
\|\widehat D_t\|
\le
G:=
\left(
\sum_{i=1}^N G_i^2
\right)^{1/2}
$ almost surely. 
Consequently,
$\|M_{t+1}\|\le \|\hat{D}_t\| + \|D_{\Phi}^{\delta_t}(x_t)\| \leq G + \sqrt{N}$ almost surely. Therefore, 
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{t=1}^{+\infty} \mathbb{E}[\|\eta_tM_{t+1}\|^2|\mathcal{F}_t] \leq (G + \sqrt{N})^2 \sum_{t=1}^{+\infty} \eta_t^2 < \infty. 
\end{equation*}
Since $\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\eta_t^2<\infty$ by Assumption~\ref{as:rm-stepsizes}, 
the vector-valued martingale series 
$\sum_{t=1}^T\eta_t M_{t+1}$ 
converges almost surely. Noting that $e_{t+1} = - M_{t+1}$, it follows that
$\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\eta_t e_{t+1}$ 
converges almost surely, as required by
Assumption~\ref{as:bias-and-stoch-error}. 
We henceforth fix a sample path in the probability-one event on
which the preceding martingale series converges and the uniform
bounds hold. 

Recall from (\ref{eq:limits-r_t-y_t-x_t}) that $r_t\to0$.  
Furthermore, recall that by construction,
$x_{i,t}=
\mathsf Q_{i,\delta_t}(z_{i,t}),$ 
and hence
$\|x_{i,t}-z_{i,t}\|
\le
\frac{R_i}{\rho_i}\delta_t.$ 
Therefore, with
$C_{\mathcal X}
:=
\left(
\sum_{i=1}^N
\frac{R_i^2}{\rho_i^2}
\right)^{1/2}$, 
we have
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:x-z-asymptotic2}
\|x_t-z_t\|
\le
C_{\mathcal X}\delta_t
\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
Hence, we obtain using (\ref{eq:limits-r_t-y_t-x_t}) and (\ref{eq:x-z-asymptotic2}), 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:state-y-to-zt-below}
\|y_t-z_t\|
\le
\|y_t-x_t\|+\|x_t-z_t\| \le \varepsilon_t + C_{\mathcal{X}} \delta_t
\longrightarrow0\,,
\end{equation}
where the limit follows from using $\varepsilon_t \to 0, \delta_t \to 0$. 
Thus Assumptions~\ref{as:bias-and-stoch-error} and
\ref{as:state-y-to-zt} are satisfied.

Assumption~\ref{as:bounded-error} also holds as we have shown above that 
$\sup_t\|M_{t+1}\|<\infty$ almost surely.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:lyapunov-ineq}
For every $i\in[N]$ define
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:psi_i}
\Psi_i(x)
:=
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle
\|g_i(x)\| \cdot 
\left\|
\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}
\left(
-\frac{g_i(x)}{\|g_i(x)\|}
\right)
\right\|^2,
&
\text{if} \,\,g_i(x)\neq0,
\\[3ex]
0,
&
\text{if} \,\, g_i(x)=0,
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
and let $U(x)
:=
-\sum_{i=1}^N\Psi_i(x).$ 
Then $U:\mathcal X\to\mathbb R$ is upper semicontinuous on $\mathcal X$, $U\le0$, and for
every $x\in\mathcal X$ and every $u\in F(x)$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:lyap-ineq}
\left\langle
\nabla\Phi(x),
\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X}(x)}u
\right\rangle
=
U(x).
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:identification-zero-set}
\{x\in\mathcal X:U(x)=0\}
=
\left\{
x\in\mathcal X:
-\nabla\Phi(x)\in N_{\mathcal X}(x)
\right\}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
\noindent\textbf{Proof of (\ref{eq:lyap-ineq}).}
Let $x\in\mathcal X$ and $w\in F_{\mathcal X}(x).$ 
By definition of the projected field, there exists
$u\in F(x)=-\mathcal D(x)$ such that
$w=\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X}(x)}(u).$ 
Since both the action set and tangent cone are Cartesian products,
this means blockwise that
$w_i =\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}(u_i),
i\in[N].$ 

Observe first that 
\begin{equation*}
\langle\nabla\Phi(x),w\rangle
= \sum_{i=1}^N \langle g_i(x), w_i\rangle\,.
\end{equation*}
To prove (\ref{eq:lyap-ineq}), we will show in what follows that for every $i \in [N]$, 
\begin{equation}
 \langle g_i(x), w_i\rangle = - \Psi_i(x)\,.
\end{equation}

Consider first a block $i \in [N]$ such that $g_i(x)\neq0$.
Then $F_i(x)$ is a singleton and necessarily
$u_i=-\frac{g_i(x)}{\|g_i(x)\|}.$ 

For a cone $C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_i}$, let
$C^\circ := \{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d_i} :
\langle v,w\rangle \leq 0,\ \forall w\in C\}$ 
denote its polar cone. For a closed convex cone $C$, Moreau's decomposition gives
\[
u_i=\Pi_C(u_i)+\Pi_{C^\circ}(u_i),
\qquad
\langle \Pi_C(u_i),\Pi_{C^\circ}(u_i)\rangle=0.
\]
Taking
$C=T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i), C^\circ=N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)$, 
and recalling that $w_i=\Pi_C(u_i)$, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
\langle u_i,w_i\rangle = \langle \Pi_C(u_i)+\Pi_{C^\circ}(u_i),\Pi_C(u_i)\rangle  
= \|\Pi_C(u_i)\|^2 = \|w_i\|^2\,.
\end{equation*}
Since $g_i(x)=-\|g_i(x)\|u_i$, 
it follows that
\begin{align}
\langle g_i(x),w_i\rangle
&=
-\|g_i(x)\|
\langle u_i,w_i\rangle
\nonumber\\
&=
-\|g_i(x)\|\cdot\|w_i\|^2
\nonumber\\
&=
-\|g_i(x)\| \cdot 
\left\|
\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}
\left(
-\frac{g_i(x)}{\|g_i(x)\|}
\right)
\right\|^2
\nonumber\\
&=
-\Psi_i(x).
\label{eq:block-lie-dissipation}
\end{align}

If instead $g_i(x)=0$, the block $u_i$ may be any element of
$-\overline{\mathbb B}_{d_i}$, but independently of this choice,
\[
\langle g_i(x),w_i\rangle=0=-\Psi_i(x).
\]
Summing over the blocks gives
\[
\langle\nabla\Phi(x),w\rangle
=
-\sum_{i=1}^N\Psi_i(x)
=
U(x).
\]
The right-hand side is independent of the particular selection
$u\in F(x)$ at zero-gradient blocks. Consequently, 
$\left\langle
\nabla\Phi(x),
\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X}(x)}u
\right\rangle
=
U(x).$ Since $\Psi_i(x)\ge0$ for every $i$, we also have $U(x)\le0$.\\

\noindent\textbf{Proof of upper semicontinuity of $U$.}
Since $U=-\sum_{i=1}^N\Psi_i$, it suffices to show that each $\Psi_i$ is lower semicontinuous to conclude that their negative sum is upper semicontinuous, i.e., for every $x^n \to x$, 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:psi-i-lower-semicont}
\Psi_i(x) \leq \liminf_n \Psi_i(x^n)\,.
\end{equation}

First, by definition, $\Psi_i(y) \geq 0$ for every $y \in \mathcal{X}$. If $g_i(x) = 0$, then $\Psi_i(x) = 0$ by definition (see (\ref{eq:psi_i})). Therefore for any such $x$ for which $g_i(x) = 0$, (\ref{eq:psi-i-lower-semicont}) holds since $0 = \Psi_i(x) \leq \liminf_n \Psi_i(x^n)\,.$ 

It remains to show lower semicontinuity of $\Psi_i$ whenever $g_i(x)\neq0$. Positive homogeneity of the projection onto a cone yields: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:alternative-expression-psi-i}
\Psi_i(x)
= \|g_i(x)\| \cdot 
\left\|
\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}
\left(
-\frac{g_i(x)}{\|g_i(x)\|}
\right)
\right\|^2
= \frac{\left\|
\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}
\left(
-g_i(x)
\right)
\right\|^2}{\|g_i(x)\|}\,.
\end{equation}
Using Moreau's decomposition gives: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:dist-to-normal-cone}
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
-g_i(x),N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)
\right) = \left\| - g_i(x) - \Pi_{\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}(- g_i(x)) \right\|
= \left\|\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}
\left(
-g_i(x)
\right)\right\|\,.
\end{equation}
Combining (\ref{eq:alternative-expression-psi-i}) and (\ref{eq:dist-to-normal-cone}) yields: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:Psi-normal-distance}
\Psi_i(x)
=
\frac{
\operatorname{dist}^2
\left(
-g_i(x),N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)
\right)
}{
\|g_i(x)\|
}.
\end{equation}
We now use this expression to show that $\Psi_i$ is lower semicontinuous in the case where $g_i(x) \neq 0$. Noting that $g_i$ is continuous, our main task is to show that the function $d_i: \mathcal{X} \to \mathbb{R}_+$ defined for any $x \in \mathcal{X}$ by:  
\[
d_i(x)
:=
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
-g_i(x),N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)
\right)
\]
is lower semicontinuous. Note here, that this is not a simple distance function to a fixed convex set which is known to be continuous. Here the set $\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{X}_i}(x_i)$ also depends on the variable $x_i.$ We prove the lower semicontinuity claim below. 

Let $x^n\to x$ and, after passing to
a subsequence realizing $\liminf_n d_i(x^n)$, let 
\[
v_i^n
:=
\Pi_{N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i^n)}
\bigl(-g_i(x^n)\bigr).
\]
Since $0\in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i^n)$,
$\|v_i^n\|\le\|g_i(x^n)\|$ by nonexpansiveness of the projection operator. As a consequence, since $(g(x^n))$ converges by continuity of $g$, it follows that it is bounded and hence that $(v_i^n)$ is also bounded. 
We can then pass to a further subsequence and let $v_i^n\to v_i$. 
The normal-cone mapping of a closed convex set has closed graph, and since $v_i^n \in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i^n)$, we obtain  
$v_i\in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)$. Therefore,
\[
d_i(x)
= \inf_{y_i \in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)} \|- g_i(x) - y_i\|
\le
\|-g_i(x)-v_i\|
= \lim_{n \to \infty} \|-g_i(x^n) - v_i^n\| 
=
\liminf_{n\to\infty} d_i(x^n)\,,
\]
where the penultimate identity follows from continuity of $g_i$ and $x^n \to x, v_i^n \to v_i$, and the last identity follows from recalling that $x^n$ is a subsequence realizing $\liminf_n d_i(x^n)$. 
We have proved that $d_i$ is lower semicontinuous. 

We now conclude to show that $\Psi_i$ as expressed in (\ref{eq:Psi-normal-distance}) is lower semicontinuous. 
We use the following elementary fact: 

\begin{lemma}
\label{lem:liminf}
If $a_n\geq 0$ and $b_n\to b>0$, then
$\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{b_n}
=\frac{\liminf_{n\to\infty}a_n}{b}.$ 
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $\liminf_n a_n=+\infty$, the claim follows immediately from
$b_n\to b>0$. Hence assume that $\liminf_n a_n<+\infty$. 
We prove the two inequalities separately.

For the first inequality, let $(a_{n_k})$ be a subsequence such that
$a_{n_k}\to \liminf_{n\to\infty}a_n.$ 
Since $b_{n_k}\to b>0$, we have
$\frac{a_{n_k}}{b_{n_k}}
\longrightarrow
\frac{\liminf_{n\to\infty}a_n}{b}.$ 
Since the liminf of a sequence is no larger than any of its
subsequential limits,
\[
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{b_n}
\le
\frac{\liminf_{n\to\infty}a_n}{b}.
\]

For the reverse inequality, let
$\bigl(a_{n_k}/b_{n_k}\bigr)$ be a subsequence such that
$\frac{a_{n_k}}{b_{n_k}}
\longrightarrow
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{b_n}.$
Since $b_{n_k}\to b>0$,
\[
a_{n_k}
=
b_{n_k}\frac{a_{n_k}}{b_{n_k}}
\longrightarrow
b\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{b_n}.
\]
Again using that the liminf is no larger than any subsequential limit,
\[
\liminf_{n\to\infty}a_n
\le
b\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{b_n}.
\]
Since $b>0$,
$\frac{\liminf_{n\to\infty}a_n}{b}
\le
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{b_n}.$ 
Combining the two inequalities proves the claim.
\end{proof}

Take now $a_n = d_i(x^n)^2, b_n = \|g_i(x^n)\|$. Since $g_i(x) \neq 0$ and $g_i$ is continuous, it follows that $b_n \to \|g_i(x)\| > 0$. By lower semicontinuity of $d_i$, $d_i(x)^2 \leq (\liminf_{n \to \infty} d_i(x^n))^2 = \liminf_{n \to \infty} d_i(x^n)^2\,.$ Therefore, we can conclude using Lemma~\ref{lem:liminf}: 
\begin{equation}
\Psi_i(x) = \frac{d_i(x)^2}{\|g_i(x)\|} \leq \frac{\liminf_{n \to \infty} d_i(x^n)^2}{\|g_i(x)\|} 
= \liminf_{n \to \infty} \frac{d_i(x^n)^2}{\|g_i(x^n)\|} =  \liminf_{n \to \infty} \Psi_i(x^n)\,,
\end{equation}
which shows that $\Psi_i$ is lower semicontinuous.
Consequently, $U=-\sum_{i=1}^N\Psi_i$ is upper semicontinuous.\\ 

\noindent\textbf{Proof of (\ref{eq:identification-zero-set}): zeros of the Lyapunov upper bound.} Since each $\Psi_i(x)\ge0$,
$U(x)=0$ if and only if $\Psi_i(x)=0$ for all $i \in [N].$

If $g_i(x)=0$, then $-g_i(x)=0\in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)$ 
and $\Psi_i(x)=0$ by definition.

Suppose now that $g_i(x)\neq0$. Then
$\Psi_i(x)=0$ 
if and only if
$
\Pi_{T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)}
\left(
-\frac{g_i(x)}{\|g_i(x)\|}
\right)
=0.$

For a closed convex cone $C$,
$\Pi_C(v)=0$ if and only if $v\in C^\circ$.
Since $T_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)^\circ
= N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)$, 
we obtain
$\Psi_i(x)=0$ if and only if $-\frac{g_i(x)}{\|g_i(x)\|}
\in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i).$ 
Since the normal cone is a cone, this is equivalent to
$-g_i(x)\in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i).$ 
This proves (\ref{eq:identification-zero-set}). 
The product-space characterization follows from noting that $N_{\mathcal X}(x)
=\prod_{i=1}^N N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i).$
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:liminf}
If $a_n\geq 0$ and $b_n\to b>0$, then
$\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{b_n}
=\frac{\liminf_{n\to\infty}a_n}{b}.$
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
If $\liminf_n a_n=+\infty$, the claim follows immediately from
$b_n\to b>0$. Hence assume that $\liminf_n a_n<+\infty$. 
We prove the two inequalities separately.

For the first inequality, let $(a_{n_k})$ be a subsequence such that
$a_{n_k}\to \liminf_{n\to\infty}a_n.$ 
Since $b_{n_k}\to b>0$, we have
$\frac{a_{n_k}}{b_{n_k}}
\longrightarrow
\frac{\liminf_{n\to\infty}a_n}{b}.$ 
Since the liminf of a sequence is no larger than any of its
subsequential limits,
\[
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{b_n}
\le
\frac{\liminf_{n\to\infty}a_n}{b}.
\]

For the reverse inequality, let
$\bigl(a_{n_k}/b_{n_k}\bigr)$ be a subsequence such that
$\frac{a_{n_k}}{b_{n_k}}
\longrightarrow
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{b_n}.$
Since $b_{n_k}\to b>0$,
\[
a_{n_k}
=
b_{n_k}\frac{a_{n_k}}{b_{n_k}}
\longrightarrow
b\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{b_n}.
\]
Again using that the liminf is no larger than any subsequential limit,
\[
\liminf_{n\to\infty}a_n
\le
b\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{b_n}.
\]
Since $b>0$,
$\frac{\liminf_{n\to\infty}a_n}{b}
\le
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{b_n}.$ 
Combining the two inequalities proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:potential-stationarity-ne}
Under Assumption~\ref{ass:block-pseudoconvex} and
Assumption~\ref{as:non-degeneracy-ord-pot},
$\mathcal S = \operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G),$ 
where $\mathcal S=\{x\in\mathcal X:U(x)=0\}$.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
By (\ref{eq:identification-zero-set}) in Lemma~\ref{lem:lyapunov-ineq} ,
$x\in\mathcal S$ if and only if 
$-\nabla_i\Phi(x) \in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i), 
\forall i\in[N].$

We first prove that $\mathcal S\subseteq\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)$. Let $x\in\mathcal S$, $i \in [N]$. 

If $\nabla_i\Phi(x)=0,$
Assumption~\ref{as:non-degeneracy-ord-pot} gives
$\nabla_iJ_i(x)=0.$ 
Suppose for contradiction that $x_i$ is not a best response to
$x_{-i}$. Then there exists $y_i\in\mathcal X_i$ such that
$J_i(y_i,x_{-i}) < J_i(x_i,x_{-i}).$ 
By blockwise pseudoconvexity,
$\left\langle
\nabla_iJ_i(x),
y_i-x_i
\right\rangle
<0,$ contradicting $\nabla_iJ_i(x)=0$.
Thus $x_i$ is a best response.

Suppose now that
$\nabla_i\Phi(x)\neq0.$ 
By Lemma~\ref{lem:ordinal-potential-gradient-alignment},
$\nabla_iJ_i(x) =
\lambda_i(x)\nabla_i\Phi(x)$ 
for some $\lambda_i(x)>0$.
Since
$-\nabla_i\Phi(x) \in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i)$ 
and the normal cone is a cone,
\[
-\nabla_iJ_i(x)
=
-\lambda_i(x)\nabla_i\Phi(x)
\in
N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i).
\]
Equivalently,
$\left\langle
\nabla_iJ_i(x),
y_i-x_i
\right\rangle
\ge0, 
\forall y_i\in\mathcal X_i.$ 
If there were $y_i\in\mathcal X_i$ satisfying
\[
J_i(y_i,x_{-i})
<
J_i(x_i,x_{-i}),
\]
blockwise pseudoconvexity would imply
$\left\langle
\nabla_iJ_i(x),
y_i-x_i
\right\rangle
<0,$ 
a contradiction. Hence $x_i$ is a best response.
Since this holds for every $i \in [N]$,
$x\in\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G).$

Conversely, suppose
$x\in\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G).$ 
For every $i \in [N]$, $x_i$ minimizes the differentiable
function
$y_i\mapsto J_i(y_i,x_{-i})$ 
over the convex set $\mathcal X_i$. The first-order necessary
optimality condition therefore gives
$\left\langle
\nabla_iJ_i(x),
y_i-x_i
\right\rangle
\ge0, 
\forall y_i\in\mathcal X_i,$
or equivalently, 
$-\nabla_iJ_i(x)
\in
N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i).$ 

If $\nabla_iJ_i(x)=0$, nondegeneracy gives
$\nabla_i\Phi(x)=0$, and consequently
$-\nabla_i\Phi(x)=0 \in N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i).$ 

If $\nabla_iJ_i(x)\neq0$, Lemma
~\ref{lem:ordinal-potential-gradient-alignment} gives
$\nabla_iJ_i(x) = \lambda_i(x)\nabla_i\Phi(x),
\lambda_i(x)>0.$ 
Hence
\[
-\nabla_i\Phi(x)
=
\frac1{\lambda_i(x)}
\bigl(-\nabla_iJ_i(x)\bigr)
\in
N_{\mathcal X_i}(x_i).
\]
Thus the potential stationarity condition holds for every player,
so
$x\in\mathcal S.$
This proves 
$\mathcal S
=
\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G).$
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- assumption-4: body_md -->
\label{as:rm-stepsizes2} The stepsizes $(\eta_t)$ and the resolution $(\delta_t)$ satisfy: 
$\sum_{t=1}^\infty\eta_t=\infty, \sum_{t=1}^\infty\eta_t^2<\infty$ 
and  $\sum_{t=1}^\infty\eta_t\delta_t<\infty\,,$ 
with $\eta_t>0, \delta_t \in (0,\rho), \forall t \in \mathbb{N}$ and $\delta_t\to0.$
<!-- end assumption-4 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{thm:as-comparison-ne-apdx}
Let $\mathcal G$ be an ordinal potential game with potential $\Phi$. Let Assumptions~\ref{ass:block-pseudoconvex} and~\ref{as:non-degeneracy-ord-pot} hold. 
Suppose that $\Phi$ is $L$-smooth and coercive\footnote{i.e., $\Phi(x)\to+\infty$ as $\|x\|\to\infty\,.$}. 
Let $(x_t)$ be generated by (\ref{eq:cngp}) with the projection inactive, where $\widehat D_{i,t}$ is defined by
(\ref{eq:cngp}) under the gap-independent noisy
comparison model with $\nu_i\in(0,1/2]$.
Suppose that the stepsizes and comparison radii satisfy
Assumption~\ref{as:rm-stepsizes2}.
Then, with probability one,
\[
\Phi(x_t)\ \text{converges},\qquad 
\|\nabla\Phi(x_t)\|_{2,1}\to0\,, \quad \operatorname{dist}(x_t,\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G))\to0\,.
\]
If $\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)$ is finite, then there exists an
$\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)$-valued random variable $X_\infty$ such that
$x_t\to X_\infty$ almost surely. In particular, if $\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)$ is a singleton, the iterates converge to it almost surely.
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:uniform-potential-alignment-as}
Under the assumptions of Theorem~\ref{thm:as-comparison-ne}, for every player $i \in [N]$,
every $\delta>0$ and every $x \in \mathcal{X}^{\delta}$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:first-ineq-to-prove}
\left\langle
\nabla_i\Phi(x),
D_i^\delta(x)
\right\rangle
\ge
\|\nabla_i\Phi(x)\|
-
\frac{L\delta}{\kappa_{d_i}}.
\end{equation}
If in addition $\nabla_i\Phi(x)\neq0$, then $D_i(x)
=
\frac{\nabla_i\Phi(x)}
{\|\nabla_i\Phi(x)\|}$ and 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:second-ineq-to-prove}
\left\langle
\nabla_i\Phi(x),
D_i^\delta(x)-D_i(x)
\right\rangle
\ge
-\frac{L\delta}{\kappa_{d_i}}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Let $x \in \mathcal{X}, i \in [N].$ Recalling the definition of the finite resolution vector field~$D_i^{\delta}$, we have 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:inner-prod-rewriting}
\left\langle
\nabla_i\Phi(x),
D_i^\delta(x)
\right\rangle 
= \frac{1}{\kappa_{d_i}} \langle \nabla_i \Phi(x), \mathbb{E}_{U_i}[S_i^{\delta}(x,U_i) U_i] \rangle 
= \frac{1}{\kappa_{d_i}} 
\mathbb{E}_{U_i}[S_i^{\delta}(x,U_i) \langle \nabla_i \Phi(x), U_i \rangle]\,. 
\end{equation}
We will prove first that for any $u \in \mathbb{R}^{d_i}$ such that $\|u\|_2 = 1$, 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:to-prove-bias-control}
S_i^{\delta}(x,u) \langle \nabla_i \Phi(x), u \rangle \geq |\langle \nabla_i \Phi(x), u \rangle| - L \delta\,.
\end{equation}
Define for any $\delta > 0$ and any $u$ s.t. $x_i \pm \delta u \in \mathcal{X}_i$ and $\|u\|_2 = 1$ the central difference quotient: 
\begin{equation}
\Delta_{\Phi, i}^\delta(u) := 
\frac{
\Phi(x_i+\delta u,x_{-i})
-
\Phi(x_i-\delta u,x_{-i})
}{2\delta}\,.
\end{equation}
By $L$-smoothness of $\Phi$, we have: 
\begin{align}
|\Phi(x_i+\delta u,x_{-i}) - 
(\Phi(x) + \delta \langle \nabla_i \Phi(x), u \rangle)| &\leq \frac{L \delta^2}{2}\,,\\
|\Phi(x_i -\delta u,x_{-i}) - 
(\Phi(x) - \delta \langle \nabla_i \Phi(x), u \rangle)| &\leq \frac{L \delta^2}{2}\,.
\end{align}
Using both the above inequalities, it follows that: 
\begin{align} 
\label{eq:diff-delta-inner-prod} 
&|\Delta_{\Phi,i}^{\delta}(u) - \langle \nabla_i \Phi(x), u \rangle| \nonumber\\
&= \frac{1}{2 \delta}\left| \Phi(x_i+\delta u,x_{-i})-\Phi(x_i-\delta u,x_{-i}) - 2\delta \langle \nabla_i \Phi(x), u \rangle \right| \nonumber\\
&= \frac{1}{2 \delta} \left|\Phi(x_i+\delta u,x_{-i}) - (\Phi(x) + \delta \langle \nabla_i \Phi(x), u \rangle ) - (\Phi(x_i -\delta u,x_{-i}) - 
(\Phi(x) - \delta \langle \nabla_i \Phi(x), u \rangle )) \right|\nonumber\\
&\leq \frac{1}{2 \delta} \left(\frac{L \delta^2}{2} +  \frac{L \delta^2}{2} \right) = \frac{\delta L}{2}\,.
\end{align}
By the ordinal-potential property,
$S_i^\delta(x,u) = \operatorname{sgn}(\Delta_{\Phi, i}^{\delta}(u)).$
If $\operatorname{sgn}(\Delta_{\Phi,i}^{\delta}(u)) =\operatorname{sgn}(\langle \nabla_i \Phi(x), u \rangle)$,
then
$S_i^\delta(x,u)\langle \nabla_i \Phi(x), u \rangle=|\langle \nabla_i \Phi(x), u \rangle|.$
Otherwise, the signs are opposite and (\ref{eq:diff-delta-inner-prod}) implies that 
$|\langle \nabla_i \Phi(x), u \rangle|
\le
\frac{\delta L}{2},$
and hence
\begin{equation}
S_i^\delta(x,u) \langle \nabla_i \Phi(x), u \rangle
=
-|\langle \nabla_i \Phi(x), u \rangle|
\ge
|\langle \nabla_i \Phi(x), u \rangle|-L\delta.
\end{equation}
Therefore, the above inequality holds for any $u$, in both cases and even if one of the two quantities is zero, which proves (\ref{eq:to-prove-bias-control}). 
Using now (\ref{eq:inner-prod-rewriting}) and taking expectation in (\ref{eq:to-prove-bias-control}) yields: 
\begin{equation}
\left\langle
\nabla_i\Phi(x),
D_i^\delta(x)
\right\rangle  
= \frac{1}{\kappa_{d_i}} 
\mathbb{E}_{U_i}[S_i^{\delta}(x,U_i) \langle \nabla_i \Phi(x), U_i \rangle] 
\geq \frac1{\kappa_{d_i}}
\mathbb E
[|\langle \nabla_i \Phi(x) ,U_i\rangle|]
-
\frac{L\delta}{\kappa_{d_i}}\,. 
\end{equation}
Rotational invariance of the uniformly distributed random variable $U_i$ on the sphere gives
$\mathbb E[|\langle \nabla_i \Phi(x),U_i\rangle|]
= \kappa_{d_i} \cdot\|\nabla_i \Phi(x)\|$ (see Lemma 3.1 in~\citet{fu-wang-yan23minimax-spherical})
and this concludes the proof of (\ref{eq:first-ineq-to-prove}). Equation (\ref{eq:second-ineq-to-prove}) follows from recalling that $D_i(x)=
\frac{\nabla_i\Phi(x)}
{\|\nabla_i\Phi(x)\|}$ when $\nabla_i\Phi(x)\neq0$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:ordinal-potential-gradient-alignment}, which implies that $\langle \nabla_i \Phi(x),D_i(x)\rangle=\|\nabla_i \Phi(x)\|\,.$
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
We divide the proof into four steps.\\

\noindent\textbf{Step 1: Conditional Lyapunov drift.}
Since $\nabla\Phi$ is $L$-Lipschitz, we have 
\[
\Phi(x_{t+1})
\le
\Phi(x_t)
+
\left\langle
\nabla\Phi(x_t),x_{t+1} - x_t
\right\rangle
+
\frac{L}{2}
\|x_{t+1}-x_t\|^2.
\]
Using the update rule of the algorithm, it follows that: 
\begin{equation}
\Phi(x_{t+1})
\le
\Phi(x_{t})
-
\eta_t
\left\langle
\nabla\Phi(x_{t}),\widehat D_t
\right\rangle
+
\frac{L\eta_t^2}{2}
\|\widehat D_t\|^2.
\end{equation}
Taking conditional expectation with respect to $\mathcal F_t$ and using Lemma~\ref{lem:one-bit-estimator} and
the definition of $D_i^{\delta}$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:master}
\mathbb E[
\Phi(x_{t+1})\mid\mathcal F_t
]
\leq 
\Phi(x_{t})
-
\eta_t
\sum_{i=1}^N
\left\langle
\nabla_i\Phi(x_t),
D_i^{\delta_t}(x_t)
\right\rangle 
+ \frac{LC_{2,\nu}}{2}\eta_t^2\,,
\end{equation}
where $C_{2,\nu} = \sum_{i=1}^N\frac1{(2\nu_i\kappa_{d_i})^2}.$

Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform-potential-alignment-as} yields
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:bias-control}
\sum_{i=1}^N
\left\langle
\nabla_i\Phi(x_t),
D_i^{\delta_t}(x_t)
\right\rangle
\ge
\|\nabla\Phi(x_t)\|_{2,1}
-
LC_1\delta_t\,, 
\end{equation}
where $C_1 = \sum_{i=1}^N\frac1{\kappa_{d_i}}.$
Hence, combining (\ref{eq:master}) with (\ref{eq:bias-control}), we obtain: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:master-lyap-drift}
\mathbb E[
\Phi(x_{t+1})\mid\mathcal F_t
]
\leq 
\Phi(x_{t})
-
\eta_t
\|\nabla\Phi(x_{t})\|_{2,1}
+
LC_1\eta_t\delta_t
+
\frac{LC_{2,\nu}}{2}\eta_t^2.
\end{equation}

\noindent\textbf{Step 2: Application of Robbins-Siegmund's theorem.}
Since $\Phi$ is continuous and coercive, it is bounded below by $\Phi_\star=\inf_{x\in\mathcal X}\Phi(x)>-\infty.$
Set
\begin{equation}
V_t :=\Phi(x_t)-\Phi_\star\ge0\,, \quad a_t :=LC_1\eta_t\delta_t
+\frac{LC_{2,\nu}}{2}\eta_t^2\,,\quad  \forall t \geq 0\,. 
\end{equation}
We can then rewrite (\ref{eq:master-lyap-drift}) as follows: 
\begin{equation}
\mathbb E[V_{t+1}\mid\mathcal F_t] \le V_t -
\eta_t \|\nabla\Phi(x_t)\|_{2,1} + a_t.
\end{equation}

By Assumption~\ref{as:rm-stepsizes2}, $\sum_{t=1}^\infty a_t<\infty$
and we can apply the Robbins-Siegmund almost supermartingale theorem \citep{robbins-siegmund71} to obtain that
$V_t$ converges almost surely to a finite random variable and that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:summability-eta-grad}
\sum_{t=1}^\infty
\eta_t
\|\nabla\Phi(x_t)\|_{2,1}
<
\infty
\qquad\text{a.s.}
\end{equation}

Since $\Phi(x_t)$ converges (as $V_t$ does) and $\Phi$ is coercive, the sequence
of play $(x_t)$ is almost surely bounded.\\

\noindent\textbf{Step 3: First-order stationarity guarantee.}
Define for any $x \in \mathcal{X}, t \geq 1$, 
\[
r(x)
:=
\|\nabla\Phi(x)\|_{2,1}
=
\sum_{i=1}^N
\|\nabla_i\Phi(x)\|,
\qquad
r_t :=r(x_t).
\]
Since 
$\sum_t\eta_t=\infty$ by Assumption~\ref{as:rm-stepsizes2} and $\sum_{t=1}^\infty \eta_t\|\nabla\Phi(x_t)\|_{2,1}<\infty$ as shown in (\ref{eq:summability-eta-grad}), it follows that 
$\liminf_{t\to\infty}r_t=0.$

We show that in fact $r_t\to0$. The proof is similar to the proof of $\lim_{t\to\infty}\|\nabla_i\Phi(x(t))\|=0$ in continuous time in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:cv-BNPG-ordinal-pot}. However, here, we deal with the discrete time setting and we provide a complete proof using again standard arguments. 

First, by the reverse triangle
inequality and the Lipschitz continuity of $\nabla\Phi$, for any $x, y \in \mathcal{X},$
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:lip}
|r(x)-r(y)|
\le \sum_{i=1}^N \|\nabla_i\Phi(x)-\nabla_i\Phi(y)\|
\le \sqrt N\,
\|\nabla\Phi(x)-\nabla\Phi(y)\|
\le \sqrt N L\|x-y\|.
\end{equation}
Furthermore, using again the update rule of the algorithm, we have: 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:increments-xt-bound}
\|x_{t+1}-x_t\| = \eta_t \|\hat{D}_t\| = \eta_t \left\|\left(\frac{Y_{i,t}}{2 \nu_i \kappa_{d_i}} U_{i,t}\right)_{i \in [N]} \right\| \leq 
\sqrt{C_{2,\nu}}\,\eta_t.
\end{equation}
Combining the above inequality with (\ref{eq:lip}) yields for any $t \geq 1$,  
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:stability}
|r_{t+1}-r_t|
\le
K\eta_t,
\qquad
K:=L\sqrt{NC_{2,\nu}}.
\end{equation}

Suppose, towards a contradiction, that
$\limsup_{n\to\infty}r_n>0.$
Together with $\liminf_{t\to\infty}r_t=0$, this implies that there exists
$\varepsilon>0$ and infinitely many disjoint upcrossings from below
$\varepsilon$ to above $2\varepsilon$. 
Consider one such sufficiently late upcrossing. Let $p<q$ be chosen so
that $r_p<\varepsilon, r_q\ge2\varepsilon,$
and $r_n\ge\varepsilon$ for $p<n\le q$.
Using (\ref{eq:stability}), we have 
\begin{equation*}
\varepsilon
< r_q-r_p
\le K\sum_{t=p}^{q-1}\eta_t.
\end{equation*}
Since $\eta_t\to0$, for all sufficiently large $p$, $K \eta_p\le\frac{\varepsilon}{2}$ which implies, using the above inequality, that 
\begin{equation}
\sum_{t=p+1}^{q-1}\eta_t
\ge
\frac{\varepsilon}{2K}\,,
\end{equation}
up to adjustments of the endpoints in the sum. On this interval,
$r_t\ge\varepsilon$, and hence
\[
\sum_{t=p+1}^{q-1}\eta_t r_t
\ge
\frac{\varepsilon^2}{2K}.
\]
Infinitely many disjoint upcrossings would therefore force
$\sum_t\eta_t r_t=\infty,$ contradicting (\ref{eq:summability-eta-grad}). Thus
$r_t\to0$ almost surely.\\ 

\noindent\textbf{Step 4: Identification of the limit set with Nash equilibrium.} The proof follows similar lines to the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:potential-stationarity-ne} and the end of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:as-comparison-ne}. 

Since the sequence $(\Phi(x_t))$ converges and $\Phi$ is coercive, $(x_t)$ is bounded. If $\operatorname{dist}\bigl(x_t,\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)
\bigr) \not\to 0$, there exists a converging subsequence $x_{t_k} \to \bar{x}$ remaining a positive distance from $\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)$. Since $\|\nabla\Phi(x_{t_k})\|_{2,1} \to 0$, $\nabla_i \Phi(\bar{x}) = 0$ for every $i \in [N]$ by continuity of $\nabla_i \Phi$. Assumption~\ref{as:non-degeneracy-ord-pot} then gives $\nabla_i J_i(\bar{x}) = 0$ for all $i \in [N]$, and Assumption~\ref{ass:block-pseudoconvex} implies that $\bar{x} \in \operatorname{NE}(\mathcal{G}),$ a contradiction. Hence  $\operatorname{dist}\bigl(x_t,\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal G)
\bigr) \rightarrow 0$. 

Moreover, $\|x_{t+1} - x_t\| \leq 
\sqrt{C_{2,\nu}}\,\eta_t$ by (\ref{eq:increments-xt-bound}). 
If $\operatorname{NE}(\mathcal{G})$ is finite, the same separation argument used at the end of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:as-comparison-ne} prevents the iterates from switching infinitely often between neighborhoods of distinct equilibria. Hence $x_t \rightarrow X_{\infty} \in \operatorname{NE}(\mathcal{G})$ almost surely.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\label{thm:comparison-potential-descent}
Suppose $\Phi$ is $L$-smooth and bounded below by $\Phi_{\inf}$. Then for any time horizon $T > 0$, 
with constant stepsize $\eta_t$ and comparison radius $\delta_t$ of order $T^{-1/2}$, 
a uniformly sampled iterate $x_{\tau}$ from $\{x_1, \ldots, x_T\}$ satisfies
$\mathbb E\|\nabla\Phi(x_\tau)\|_{2,1}
=\mathcal O(T^{-1/2}).$
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- theorem-10: body_md -->
\label{thm:comparison-potential-descent-apdx}
Suppose $\Phi$ is $L$-smooth and bounded below by $\Phi_{\inf}$ along
the iterates of the algorithm. Then for any time horizon $T > 0$, 
\begin{equation}
\mathbb E
\|\nabla\Phi(x_\tau)\|_{2,1}
\le
\frac{
\Delta_\Phi
+
LC_1\sum_{t=1}^T\eta_t\delta_t
+
\frac{LC_{2,\nu}}{2}\sum_{t=1}^T\eta_t^2
}{
\sum_{t=1}^T\eta_t
}\,,
\end{equation}
where $\Delta_\Phi =\Phi(x_1)-\Phi_{\inf}.$
and $\tau$ is a random variable sampled according to
$\mathbb P(\tau=t) =
\frac{\eta_t}{\sum_{s=1}^T\eta_s}\,.$
Setting $
\eta_t=\frac{a}{\sqrt T},
\delta_t=\frac{b}{\sqrt T}$
for fixed constants $a,b>0$ yields 
$\mathbb E\|\nabla\Phi(x_\tau)\|_{2,1} = \mathcal{O}(T^{-1/2})$
where $\tau$ is uniform on
$\{1,\ldots,T\}$.
<!-- end theorem-10 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Recall from (\ref{eq:master-lyap-drift}) that 
\begin{equation}
\mathbb E[
\Phi(x_{t+1})\mid\mathcal F_t
]
\leq 
\Phi(x_{t})
-
\eta_t
\|\nabla\Phi(x_{t})\|_{2,1}
+
LC_1\eta_t\delta_t
+
\frac{LC_{2,\nu}}{2}\eta_t^2.
\end{equation}
Taking total expectation in the above inequality, summing over $t = 1, \dots, T$, using the lower bound
$\Phi_{\inf}$, rearranging and dividing by $\sum_{t=1}^T\eta_t$ yields: 
\begin{equation}
\frac{\sum_{t=1}^T
\eta_t
\mathbb E
\left[
\|\nabla\Phi(x_t)\|_{2,1}
\right]}{\sum_{t=1}^T \eta_t}
\le
\frac{
\Delta_\Phi
+
LC_1
\sum_{t=1}^T
\eta_t\delta_t
+
\frac{LC_{2,\nu}}{2}
\sum_{t=1}^T
\eta_t^2}{\sum_{t=1}^T \eta_t},
\end{equation}
where
$\Delta_\Phi =\Phi(x_1)-\Phi_{\inf}.$ 
The above can be rewritten equivalently using 
a random variable $\tau$ sampled according to
$\mathbb P(\tau=t) =
\frac{\eta_t}{\sum_{s=1}^T\eta_s},$ as follows: 
\begin{equation}
\mathbb E
\|\nabla\Phi(x_\tau)\|_{2,1}
\le
\frac{
\Delta_\Phi
+
LC_1\sum_{t=1}^T\eta_t\delta_t
+
\frac{LC_{2,\nu}}{2}\sum_{t=1}^T\eta_t^2
}{
\sum_{t=1}^T\eta_t
}.
\end{equation}
